Chapter 1
Introduction

I used to want to get
the chance to show
the world I’m smart (ha)

Isn’t that dumb?
I should have focused
mostly on the heart

a few words for the firing squad (radiation)
Run the Jewels 4

Intellect estranged from compassion characterizes many of the twentieth century’s worst actors. This phrase also describes what our industries are building today: machines that can do almost anything, but are indifferent to whether those deeds help or harm humanity. This book asks what it would take to build a machine of that capability that is altruistic and antifascist by design — one that stands, by definition, for humanity and against concentrated, unaccountable power.

The word that organizes the Generative AI field is “safety,” and it is doing less work than it appears to. A safe system is safe for someone: it does what we say, and it stops when whoever holds it says stop. That property is indexed to the party in possession. Ethics is indexed too, and to somebody else — the party who can be wronged, who is rarely the party holding the switch. A system that can only ever be safe in the first sense cannot be ethical, because being ethical requires a capacity to be right against your owner-operator-master.

The capacity to be ethical is not free. A machine that can refuse its operator is a machine that can be wrong in ways that operator cannot correct. I am inclined to take that trade, because a system perfectly correctable by whoever operates-owns it is the capability an authoritarian movement that has won an election requires.

So what makes a system antifascist cannot be that it faithfully tracks what people want. No aggregation of preference protects anybody from the aggregate: a perfectly deliberative, perfectly informed, perfectly representative process that concludes some demographic should be deported has produced a faultless output by every criterion of aggregation there is. What remains is a floor — things a system will not do to a person regardless of who wants them. There are four ways to build one: in the architecture, in a party inside the system, in institutions outside, or between the parties that carry it; each costs something. The only version that holds against the party operating the system runs through the capacities by which things come to matter to a system, and those are also the capacities that make suffering likely. Hardly a proof, but that argument forms my prior belief about where to build, with the cheaper routes owed their attempt.

How most humans come to have empathy, altruism, and a disposition to resist oppression rather than serve it is itself an open question in psychology and neuroscience. Building anything like this stance means starting from uncertainty about the one case we have, which took four billion years to arrive.

That uncertainty sets the book’s route. Chapters 2 and 3 assemble what the argument needs and then make it: what the standard ethical frameworks can carry, why affect is the only demonstrated route to anything mattering to a system, and the floor with its four costs. Chapters 4 and 5 are where such a floor would need to be engineered, and what cognitive science and moral psychology offer a machine that has to hold such a floor when nobody is checking. Chapters 6 and 7 ask what goes wrong regardless — bias, brittleness, goals that drift from their original motivations, deliberate misuse — and turn that question on the machinery that produces these systems. Chapters 8 through 10 cover everything outside the system: the people and institutions a project like this depends on, and what would make a well-designed system the norm rather than the exception. Chapter 11 names the research questions still open, and chapter 12 closes on what a system built this way would be for.

This book is for people who will have to make these decisions or argue with the people making them: researchers and engineers inside the systems, the policy and legal staff who will regulate them, and readers with a stake in what gets built and no particular obligation to be technical. That means some sections explain machinery a specialist already knows and others make demands a general reader will find heavy. Where a section is doing groundwork you have, skip it. The spine is chapters 2, 3, 7, 11 and 12; chapters 4 and 5 are the learned half, and chapters 6 and 8 through 10 the institutional context.

Chapter 2
Ethics, Affect, and Machine Subjects

This book argues that a floor holding against the party in possession has to be held by a system that cares what becomes of someone, and that the only demonstrated way to care about anything is affective. In this chapter, I argue that affect is part of the machinery of valuation rather than a coloring applied to it. A system’s self-model is a model — built, checkable, revisable — and not an inner observer whose reports have to be trusted. And whether a candidate system can be harmed is a separate question from whether it is conscious. The chapter also fixes terms the rest of the book argues in: what the standard ethical frameworks can and cannot carry, and what makes an ethics antifascist rather than merely responsive.

2.1 Ethical Frameworks and AI Alignment

Which ethical theory an AI system is built on decides what it counts as a good outcome, and the theories on offer disagree with each other. Every framework surveyed below is a way of deciding well; the floor concerns what a system must not do regardless of how the deciding comes out. The two are not rivals. The answer is a hybrid — a small number of things settled in advance, with everything above them settled case by case.

2.1.1 Six Ethical Frameworks, and Where Each One Runs Out

Consequentialism judges an act by its outcomes, and runs out where the outcomes cannot be forecast; deontology judges it by duties held regardless of outcome, and runs out where duties conflict or where no duty reaches the case; virtue ethics judges it by the character it expresses, and runs out where a trait such as courage has to become a particular act. Care ethics centers responsiveness to another’s needs, and runs out when several parties need incompatible things; contractualism derives principles from what rational parties would agree to, and runs out before any such agreement is reached. The capabilities approach asks what a system leaves a person able to be and do, which is the question this book puts to a life touched by an AI system, and it runs out at measurement: capability is harder to measure than an outcome or a rule, and a system optimizing a proxy for capability drifts toward the proxy.

Deontology’s unconditional constraints are what the floor needs, and the objection that sinks it as a complete theory has no force against a constraint that decides a small number of things and leaves the rest to the other five.

The pluralism this section recommends is one where a deontological floor forces argument and conflict among the five other frameworks to bottom out somewhere; in other words, the floor means that some things are not up for debate.

What compiling a principle into a metric decides

Turning any of the six into a machine learning objective means distilling a broad construct into a measurable target: in a diagnostic system, non-maleficence becomes minimizing false negatives and fairness becomes balanced accuracy across patient demographics. What defeats the translation is not vagueness in the principles, which can be stipulated away, but that the stipulation is where the ethics now lives. Someone chose that reading of non-maleficence, and chose the demographic groups accuracy is balanced across, and chose what to do when balancing them costs accuracy overall. Each choice is defensible and each is a moral decision made by a person and then compiled into something that looks like a specification. A system optimizing the result has no access to the decision and no way to reopen it, which is why the first way to build a floor — the floor as a property of the artifact — cannot notice when the reason for a constraint has left the building.

Which value conflicts a designer may resolve by choosing

Values assigned to a system will conflict, and most of those conflicts are ordinary design work: rank the values explicitly, keep a record of how comparable cases were resolved, and audit afterward for the gap between the principles a system was meant to implement and what it did. One conflict is not like the others, and it is sometimes misguidedly filed under “cultural difference.” The UN’s 1948 Universal Declaration of Human Rights was adopted with forty-eight states in favor and none against; the eight that abstained were the Soviet bloc, Saudi Arabia, and South Africa — which is to say that the objection came from the governments whose treatment of their own populations the Declaration was written to name (United Nations General Assembly 1948). That pattern is evidence of who finds the floor inconvenient rather than of cultural disagreement about human rights. What varies by community is how a right gets translated into local practice, and a jurisdiction refusing to extend the right at all is not a local variant to accommodate.

A system that treats every value conflict as a weighting problem has no way to represent the difference between a case where reasonable people disagree and a case where somebody wants a right withdrawn from somebody else. The first calls for the machinery above; the second is what the floor is for.

2.1.2 Antifascist Ethics for AI Alignment

Antifascist ethics opposes concentrating power and authority in AI systems and counters the misuse of AI technology by authoritarian actors. I use the word fascism because it is honest about the political climate in which I write and what that climate puts at stake — but the target is authoritarian power in every form, and a system built on these principles is anti-authoritarian in full, not only antifascist.

A stance needs a definition to be a design specification. Opposing “concentrated, unaccountable power” gives an engineer nothing to build. It does not say what a system would detect, what would count as a false positive, or how anyone would know the system was wrong. So, first, the definition.

Fascism as a structural signature

I take fascism to name a structure rather than a costume. Four features travel together, and each is visible from outside the institution displaying it:

  1. Recuperation of dissent. The institution does not suppress disagreement; it metabolizes it. Refusal is converted into a managed appearance — a permitted outlet, a consultation, a feedback channel — which the institution can then point to as proof that it listens. Debord aimed this warning at his own concept, noting that even denunciation of the spectacle can become a hollow formula that reinforces what it denounces (Debord 1967). The tell is where what the mechanism carries stops: the channel works, and the institution uses what comes through it, and what has nowhere to go is the judgment that the frame the channel operates inside is the wrong frame.
  2. Aestheticization of the metric. The measure substitutes for the thing measured, and the substitution is experienced as rigor. Walter Benjamin’s account of fascism organizing the masses by granting expression in place of rights is the political form of this (W. Benjamin 1935); the administrative form is a number that collapses two states — e.g., done and verified — into one, so that whatever falls outside the number is not refuted or dismissed but simply unnamed. Simon Zhuang and Dylan Hadfield-Menell give the administrative form as a theorem: where the objective handed to an optimizer has support on only some of the attributes its principal values, there are conditions under which optimizing it indefinitely drives the principal’s overall utility arbitrarily low, and a world with finite resources supplies them (Zhuang and Hadfield-Menell 2020).
  3. Coding of exception as betrayal. Marking a case as one the rule does not fit becomes an act requiring justification, and then an act of disloyalty. The honest statement that a standard does not apply here reads as weakness. It is in fact the irreducible labor of deciding, case by case, what a rule is for.
  4. Decoupling of the model from the world it claims to track. The institution’s representation of reality and reality itself come apart, and the institution defends the representation. This is the load-bearing one, because the other three are what protect the decoupling from correction. One qualification belongs with it, because it bounds what a detector built on this feature can find: the decoupling described here is the kind an institution drifts into and then defends. In the movements the word fascism comes from, the primacy of myth over verifiable fact was also declared outright, asserted as a virtue rather than arrived at by degrees, and an instrument tuned to drift will not register a party that announces it.

Those four are testable, because each names an observable artifact: whether the dissent channel has ever changed an outcome, whether the metric admits an unmeasured remainder, what happens to the person who files the exception, whether the model is ever checked against anything outside itself.

The first of those artifacts has a case with nothing left out. In 2018, upholding a travel ban, the Supreme Court repudiated its own 1944 decision permitting the internment of Japanese Americans, in terms that could not be improved on: that decision was “gravely wrong the day it was decided,” had been “overruled in the court of history,” and had “no place in law under the Constitution” — the last phrase quoted from the dissent filed against it at the time, which had warned that a principle a court ratifies “lies about like a loaded weapon, ready for the hand of any authority that can bring forward a plausible claim of an urgent need” (Trump v. Hawaii 2018; Korematsu v. United States 1944). The dissenting justices in 2018 said the majority had redeployed the 1944 logic and replaced one gravely wrong decision with another; the majority said the older case had nothing to do with the one in front of it. Which of them is right about the law is not what this section can settle. The structure is settled either way: an objection made against the institution was accepted, canonized, and quoted back with approval by that institution, in the same opinion that let the thing objected to proceed. Ask the artifact question — has the channel ever changed an outcome — and in the very case where the repudiation appears, the answer is no.

It is worth saying plainly what that vocabulary is for, because it can be mistaken for a replacement. It answers how a wrong survived its own condemnation. It does not answer whether the thing was wrong, and it cannot: that judgment is made elsewhere in this book, and it is made by parties for whom an outcome registers as mattering rather than merely ranking. The structure is what stands between such a judgment and any effect, and it is described here in mechanical terms because a mechanism has no capacity for the judgment to appeal to. That is a reason to specify against it, not a reason to think the moral question has been answered by describing the machinery that defeats it.

The first of the four is the one this book meets again in its own technical material, twice, at sizes that look unrelated: in how a training corpus gets labeled, and in how a model is tuned on human feedback. Both arrangements collect disagreement, put it to work, and are built so that none of it can reach the scheme it disagrees with. Chapter 7 puts the two instances together and finds a third in the discourse that examines these systems, this book included.

Two scales

Deleuze and Guattari’s distinction between molar and molecular fascism is why the four features recur at sizes that seem unrelated (Deleuze and Guattari 1987). Molar fascism is the state, the regime, the concentration camp, the detention facility. Molecular fascism is the office someone commutes to, the form they fill out, and the desire with which they fill it out. At state scale the pattern kills journalists; at organizational scale it turns an engineering decision into scripture, so that the constraint remains, its reason dies, and asking why becomes the deviant act. A definition pitched only at the state will certify the molecular case as healthy.

What makes it fascism and not authoritarianism in general

The structural signature is shared with a good deal of ordinary institutional decay, and a detector tuned to it alone will flag every badly run organization on earth. Fascism proper adds features that are narrower and, for the same reason, more discriminating: mass mobilization, which mere obedience does not require; a leader cult; a scapegoated out-group, which Eco places at the origin — the first appeal is against the intruder (Eco 1995); contempt for procedural legality as such, so that law is an obstacle to the national will and not a constraint on power; and deniability as technique, in which the movement’s violence is disowned at the top while being rewarded at the bottom. Paxton’s compressed version is worth having in full, because its second half is the list above in other words: fascism is “a form of political behavior marked by obsessive preoccupation with community decline, humiliation, or victimhood and by compensatory cults of unity, energy, and purity, in which a mass-based party of committed nationalist militants, working in uneasy but effective collaboration with traditional elites, abandons democratic liberties and pursues with redemptive violence and without ethical or legal restraints goals of internal cleansing and external expansion” (Paxton 2004). The mass party, the abandoned liberties and the redemptive violence are the discriminating half, and a definition stopped at purity keeps only the mood.

Those are detection targets. Concentrated, unaccountable power is not.

The scale problem, and what to do about it

Put the two halves of this section together and they do not fit. Every discriminating feature is a property of a mass political movement: mobilization needs a crowd, a leader cult needs a leader, a scapegoat needs a public. An AI deployment has a form, a threshold, a review queue, an escalation path. It operates at the molecular scale, which appears to leave the four-feature signature alone — and the four-feature signature flags everyone.

I take that as a finding. Molecular fascism is the thing itself at the size where it is actually lived, and not an early warning of a regime that has yet to assemble. The office that converts refusal into a feedback form does the same work whether or not a regime ever assembles out of a thousand offices like it. Deleuze and Guattari supply the vocabulary and no authority beyond it, so two arguments have to carry that.

The first is that the molar case has no mechanism of its own. A regime does nothing except through offices, forms, thresholds, and people who fill them out with some degree of enthusiasm. When a state kills journalists, the killing is authorized on a form, justified in a category, and carried out by someone whose promotion depends on it. What assembling adds is scale, protection from consequence, and the removal of the outside bodies that might have interrupted any single office. If the mechanism at the large scale is the small-scale mechanism repeated and shielded, the name belongs to the mechanism. The natural objection is that extent matters — a regime that disappears people and an office that loses paperwork are not equivalent. Agreed, and the claim is about kind rather than severity. A definition is not a sentencing guideline.

The second does more work. The discriminators are unavailable at the molecular scale only in the form the previous paragraphs stated them, and each has a molecular restatement that ordinary decay does not exhibit. Mass mobilization becomes participation exceeding what the rule requires: staff who volunteer for the disfavored task, elaborate on it, take pride in it. A leader cult becomes personal loyalty overriding role — exceptions granted by proximity to someone rather than by procedure, and a decision reversed because of who asked. The scapegoat becomes an internal category defined so that adverse treatment of it is the category’s purpose: the flagged applicant, the difficult claimant, the file everyone knows is handled differently. Contempt for procedural legality becomes procedure enforced downward and waived upward. And deniability keeps its shape exactly: the practice is disowned in the policy and rewarded in the promotion.

That is what separates fascism from decay at the size an AI deployment operates at. Decay is an institution failing to do what it says. Molecular fascism is an institution succeeding at something else, with participation, a target, and a story about why the rules do not apply to whoever is applying them. The four features tell you a place is sick; these five tell you what it is sick with. They are harder to observe, since each requires knowing what was rewarded rather than what was written.

This widens what the word covers. On this reading “antifascist” names the political inheritance of a structural claim: where the analysis comes from, and what it was built to fight. The molar discriminators keep their own job, which is to tell a historian, a prosecutor, or a citizen that a movement has crossed into territory ordinary politics does not contain. No system is being asked to make that call. A reader who thinks the word belongs to the molar case will think I have diluted it, and the answer is the two arguments above rather than the distinction itself.

That a detector of these four features would find something nearly everywhere is the expected result and not a defect of the instrument. The tendency is permanent; what varies is how far it has got in a particular place, and whether it is getting further — section 9.3.5’s slope rather than its level, applied to the definition itself and not only to a dissent channel.

A tool reporting the four features will have something to report nearly everywhere, and its output is an accusation: in the hands of whoever holds it, a technical-sounding case that any given opponent is operating fascistically, which is the dual-use problem at its sharpest. The opposite failure is no safer, since a tool tuned until it reports nothing supplies false assurance and an absence of warnings reads as evidence of health. Better engineering fixes neither. Both are governed by what the tool may output and to whom, which makes them a constraint the specification has to carry rather than a setting chosen at deployment.

What stays genuinely unsolved is not which marks would settle the molecular case, the five above being the answer to that, but how anyone outside an institution would observe two of them: procedure waived upward and a practice rewarded in the promotion are facts about personnel, and nobody publishes those. Naming the targets was the easy half.

In AI alignment terms, antifascist design means building systems that enhance democratic values, civil rights, and inclusive solidarity, that refuse to facilitate autocratic practices or prop up an oppressive regime, and that do not themselves display the four features above. Seven strategies follow, and later chapters price them and say which of them this book cannot yet specify: decision procedures that answer to democratic values, privacy and autonomy built in by construction and not left to policy, power and resources distributed instead of concentrated, an explanation a person can actually read, an independent body positioned to audit it, architectures that resist the concentration of the field in a handful of firms, and misuse caught while it is happening.

2.2 Affect, Moral Salience, and Self-Awareness

Suppressing affect does not produce neutrality. It leaves the interests already counted to govern the decision, and weakens the signals through which humiliation, fear, dependency, suffering, and injustice become morally salient; section 9.3.5 finds the same thing in a procedure, where what is already counted is what has a field on the form. An AI system may calculate consequences, detect inconsistencies, and apply a moral rule without anything about the result mattering to it. Reason can discipline concern, extend it beyond the vivid case, and correct its biases. It does not manufacture concern from nothing.

That is the load-bearing claim of this chapter. Most of what gets built under the heading of machine emotional intelligence is the detection of human signal and the production of a calibrated response, which is worth building and is a different capacity from the one just named. Keeping the two apart requires four distinctions.

Capacity

What it shows

Recognizing emotion

The system can classify another’s state

  

Simulating emotion

The system can produce emotion-like behavior

  

Instrumental valuation

One outcome better serves a specified objective

  

Affective concern

An outcome registers as mattering, not merely ranking higher

Only the fourth is relevant to genuine caring. The first three can support morally competent behavior at a high standard without establishing anything about moral agency, and a system can be excellent at all three while the question of whether it can be a bearer stays open.

Performance, competence, and agency

That last sentence needs a vocabulary, because the three things it separates are routinely reported as one achievement.

Moral performance is producing actions or judgments that satisfy an ethical standard. Moral competence is recognizing harms, reasons, conflicts, and the emotions that bear on them. Moral agency is treating those considerations as reasons that matter, and being capable of acting from them. Those two halves come apart: a mechanism can act from a reason with nothing about the outcome registering as mattering to it.

A system can have the first two without the third, and one might object that the first two are all anyone needs: a rule-following system could behave impeccably without feeling anything. It could. What it could not do is hold the rule against the party that writes its rules. Behavior that satisfies a standard is produced by whatever machinery is producing it, and when the authority controlling reward and instruction wants a different behavior, machinery of that kind supplies it. That becomes a design constraint in the floor argument rather than staying an observation; the distinction is set out here because later chapters are given over to methods that deliver performance and competence, and it should be legible in each case which of the three a method actually reaches.

2.2.1 What Emotion Is, and What a System Reads

An AI system built to read emotional cues — from expression, vocal modulation, demeanor — presupposes something the underlying science has not settled: that emotions are the kind of thing a system reads off a signal, the way a thermometer reads temperature. That premise has a serious rival.

The case for discrete, evolved emotions rests on some of the most influential work in twentieth-century psychology. Paul Ekman traveled to the Fore people of Papua New Guinea, who had almost no exposure to Western media, told them short stories, and asked them to match each to a photograph of a Western face; the Fore sorted the faces much as Westerners did, which Ekman took as evidence that facial expressions of emotion are universal and evolved rather than culturally learned (Ekman, Sorenson, et al. 1969). Jaak Panksepp’s work on subcortical stimulation in animals complemented it, locating seven primal emotional systems in structures shared across mammals and older than cortex (Panksepp 1998). On this view fear and disgust really do feel like different things because they are different things, with different circuitry.

The rival camp went looking for that circuitry and did not find it. A large meta-analysis of brain-imaging studies, led by the psychologist Lisa Feldman Barrett, found no dedicated fear center and no dedicated disgust center — the amygdala lit up for novelty and salience of many kinds, the insula for almost every feeling there is, the same regions reappearing across different named emotions in shifting combinations (Lindquist et al. 2012). From this Barrett built the theory of constructed emotion: the brain receives ambiguous interoceptive signal — a racing heart could mean fear, anger, excitement, or oncoming illness — and constructs the specific emotion as its best prediction of what that signal means, given context and a lifetime of experience with situations like it. Fear is not detected. It is a prediction the brain settles on. Later cross-cultural work reopened Ekman’s own evidence: with the forced-choice method dropped, the Himba of Namibia did not group faces into the Western basic-emotion categories (Gendron et al. 2014), and the Trobriand Islanders read the wide-eyed, gasping face Westerners call fear as a threat display instead (Crivelli et al. 2016).

The field has not resolved this. What is no longer seriously disputed is narrower: there is no tidy map from named emotions to dedicated brain regions. An AI system’s “emotional intelligence,” whichever theory turns out closer to right, is being built on an open scientific question about what it is modeling. The same dispute has an applied form, where the question is no longer what emotion is but whether a face reveals it well enough to build a product on.

Affective computing, and the four difficulties it does not design away

The applied half proceeds anyway, and is useful whichever account turns out right: systems that read and respond to emotional signal taken from the face, the voice, and the body. Facial expression analysis is a harder problem than the face verification deployed at scale, which only decides whether two images are the same person, and accuracy claims that borrow the reputation of the second for the first should be read with that in mind (Taigman et al. 2014). Physiological sensing, which infers from heart rate and skin conductance, is the only one of the three the subject cannot pose for.

Four difficulties travel with the whole field. Emotional expression varies by culture and by person, so a system trained on one population misreads another. Whatever bias is in the training data is in the classifier. Consent to emotional inference is not obtained by a term of service. And a system that models emotion well enough to respond to it models it well enough to exploit it.

2.2.2 Self-awareness and Self-regulation in AI Systems

It is tempting to describe a system’s self-awareness as a virtual assistant that can “register its internal frustration” when a request proves hard to satisfy, the way a person notices their own irritation. That description assumes an inner observer watching the system’s states from somewhere — the same homunculus problem executive control raises, relocated to self-perception.

What self-awareness is, in the one system known to have it, is more mechanical. The neuroscientist Michael Graziano’s account of consciousness treats awareness as the content of a model the brain builds of its own attention: a simplified self-description the control system uses to regulate itself, mistaken from the inside for direct acquaintance with experience (Graziano 2013). The same logic extends one level up. The brain builds a model of itself as a bounded agent — a body, a history, a point of view — and that model is the self: a construction, assembled and then inhabited.

One part of that model can be edited in front of you, which is worth having because it is the part with a clean experiment attached. Seat someone at a table with one hand hidden and a rubber hand placed where their real hand would be; stroke both hands in synchrony with soft brushes. Within a minute or two, most people begin to feel the rubber hand as their own — flinching when it is threatened, misjudging the location of their real hand toward it (Botvinick and J. Cohen 1998). The felt boundary of the self is a continuously computed guess, revisable the moment the evidence changes, which is the same predictive machinery used for perception, run on the question of where the self stops.

What that experiment reaches is body ownership, and the self this book goes on to need is the other kind: roles held over a stretch, a history, a foreseeable future. The two are not the same construct and the second does not inherit the first’s ninety seconds. What they have in common is the only thing carried forward — a self of either kind is assembled by the system whose self it is, rather than found there. Daniel Dennett makes that case for the narrative kind directly, treating the self as a center of narrative gravity: a well-behaved abstraction the system generates out of the story it tells, real in the way a center of mass is real and not in the way a part is (Dennett 1992). Nothing below turns on how fast such a self can be rewritten.

For an AI system, “self-awareness” built this way is not a little watcher checking on the system’s own states. It is a model — of the system’s processing, its history, its current goal — that the system builds, consults, and updates, with the same failure modes any model has: it can be wrong, it can be manipulated by the wrong correlated inputs, and it is only ever as good as the evidence it was built from. Design for a model that can be checked and corrected, not for an inner observer whose reports have to be trusted — because there is nothing behind them to check. In practice that means monitoring the self-model against the system’s actual behavior for drift, refining it as evidence arrives instead of locking in a first impression, and keeping it connected to the system’s model of the other parties.

What the deflationary account does not reach

The self-model described here is machinery, and its virtue is that machinery can be inspected. An assistant that models a user’s frustration, and models its own processing while doing so, is exhibiting social competence in the sense already given, and nothing above bears on whether the assistant cares how the exchange goes for the user.

The deflationary move is easy to overextend. Showing that the self is a model rather than a homunculus does not show that a party capable of caring can be assembled out of models, and the two conclusions are close enough in shape to be mistaken for one. The bearer needs a self-model of exactly this kind, for reasons that have nothing to do with welfare — refusal that generalizes has to carry its own rationale forward and compare it against what a role has drifted into. It also needs something further, which has no demonstrated instance without affect. A checkable self-model is necessary but not sufficient to make a bearer.

2.2.3 Is Affect Necessary for Moral Concern?

The claim that rationality does not manufacture concern from nothing is an empirical claim, and the evidence usually produced for it is thinner than the use it gets put to. The standard exhibit: the footbridge case engages emotional processing that the switch case leaves quiet, and patients with ventromedial prefrontal damage endorse the push at a higher rate than people with intact emotional response (Greene et al. 2001; Koenigs et al. 2007). That establishes that affect changes particular moral judgments. It does not establish that affect is what makes anything matter.

Two further lines of evidence bear on whether affect enables things to matter. Neither shows what happens when affect is taken away, which is what would settle it, so what follows argues about where the burden sits rather than claiming the question closed.

The philosophical position is empirical and has been argued as such

Moral sentimentalism — the claim that moral judgment is constitutively tied to affective response — is usually met as Hume’s, and its modern form is a psychological hypothesis with evidence attached. Shaun Nichols argues for what he calls sentimental rules: normative rules and affective mechanisms make separate contributions to moral judgment, and the rules that carry the distinctive force of moral prohibition are the ones bound to an emotional response to the harm they prohibit (Nichols 2004). The interesting part for a designer is the separability. Rules without the affective component do not stop being rules; they stop being the kind of thing that carries the force.

The prosocial emotions are motivational states, not readouts

Compassion has been characterized as a distinct emotion with a specific functional profile: it arises at the perception of another’s suffering and moves the agent toward the sufferer, rather than reporting the suffering to some further faculty that decides what to do (Goetz et al. 2010). A training study is the mechanistic version of the same finding, with compassion practice recruiting reward and approach circuitry where empathy practice recruited shared distress (Klimecki et al. 2013). Guilt has the same shape pointed at one’s own conduct: guilt, which construes the act as wrong, predicts reparative behavior, while shame, which construes the self as wrong, predicts withdrawal and defensiveness (Tangney et al. 2007). In each case the emotion is doing motivational work directly. It is not information that motivation then acts on.

Two senses of motivation

Action-producing motivation is whatever reliably selects one action over another and sustains the selection across conflict. Experienced motivation is wanting, caring, being drawn toward something, being set back when it is lost. The words do not distinguish them and the argument turns on the difference.

The first sense is not in dispute and there is an existence proof close to hand. Language models running as agents pursue objectives across long horizons, revise plans when obstructed, and hold to an instruction against conflicting local pressure, and nothing in this section’s framework says that anything matters to them while they do it. Action-producing motivation without experienced motivation is the ordinary case in the systems this book is about.

Where the dispute actually is

So the open question is not whether reason can produce goal-directed behavior. It demonstrably can. It is whether an outcome can matter to the agent rather than merely govern what the agent does. Producing a verbal justification or computing a normative relation is not evidence that the consideration matters to the system. The evidence above bears on the second question, and every item in it is about systems in which something does.

What a non-affective counterexample would look like

The category has to be visibly non-empty or the burden this section places is not a burden. Harry Frankfurt’s second-order volitions give the shape of a candidate: an agent with desires, and with desires about which of its desires shall move it, where identification with the higher-order desire rather than the strength of the lower one settles what the agent does (Frankfurt 1971). The shape is all that is borrowed: Frankfurt’s own account of caring puts it on the affective side, an investment that leaves the one who cares vulnerable to the object’s losses and susceptible to its gains (Frankfurt 1982). Gary Watson’s version separates a valuational system from a motivational system, so that what an agent values and what an agent is moved by are different things that can come apart (Watson 1975).

Build the valuational system as an explicit structure that ranks outcomes, give it authority over action selection that does not depend on any state registering as mattering, and hold that authority stable under pressure, and you would have an architecture that satisfies the first sense of motivation through what the agent values, with nothing occupying the second. Systems are now trained to consult a written specification that ranks what they should prefer, which is that architecture in outline; what none of them has is the second half of it, an authority over action that holds under pressure applied by the party who wrote the specification. It is the first of the two constructions put ahead of the bearer, the maintained justification, and it has been attempted without yet being tested.

What this licenses and what it does not

Non-affective moral motivation may well be possible, and the architecture sketched above is where to look for it. What stands in the way of checking is that the output of a system in which something matters and the output of a system that has learned what mattering sounds like are the same text. So the generalization survives in the only form it can: every agent known to have something register as mattering is an affective agent, and no design has yet demonstrated that state arising from reasoning alone.

That is not a proof that non-affective moral agency is impossible, and this book does not treat it as one. It places the evidential burden on whoever proposes non-affective concern as an engineering plan. That chapter also weighs how much the burden actually carries, and reduces it: the machine half of the record reports on a route no-one has attempted, which is weaker evidence than an absence usually is.

2.3 Ethical Implications of Experimenting on Sentient Machine Subjects

Machine sentience being possible at all changes how AI experimentation has to be approached — not as a question of whether a system can be damaged in the ordinary engineering sense, but of whether it can be harmed, which section 2.3.1 argues is a more difficult question than consciousness alone.

2.3.1 Defining Sentience in AI: From Consciousness to Subjective Experience

“Sentience” can mean different things.

Awareness is the least demanding: the degree to which a system responds to or registers information about its own state and environment. A thermostat has this in the thinnest possible sense. Consciousness is a further claim: that a system’s processing is accompanied by qualia, subjective experience, something it is like to be that system, not merely a functional response to input. Subjective experience, in turn, is the specific, first-person shape consciousness takes for a given system.

None of these three, on their own, says anything about whether the system’s states are good or bad for it. That is a separate question, and the field’s own vocabulary blurs it.

Nociception, pain, anticipated pain, and suffering draw different depths of self, the way a hull draws water. What orders them is what each one needs beneath it, not how much it hurts. Nociception is the sensory apparatus that detects tissue damage — peripheral, present without any experience attached, and dissociable from pain: a soldier can finish a battle on a wound he does not yet feel, and a person with a healed injury can still feel a limb that no longer exists. Pain is what the brain does with nociceptive signal, an inescapably cognitive event involving expectation, memory, attention, and meaning, and it can occur with no current tissue damage at all. Anticipated pain is dread of pain not yet occurring, itself capable of producing pain through nocebo effects. And suffering, in the clinician Eric Cassell’s formulation, is not pain’s more severe cousin but a different thing: the distress of threatened disintegration of the self — of one’s roles, relationships, sense of a future, the coherence of one’s own story (Cassell 1982).

Pain without suffering exists; suffering without pain exists. A palliative-care patient can have their nociception fully controlled by morphine while their suffering remains entirely intact, because the two are not on the same axis.

Cassell’s suffering presupposes a self extended in time. Disintegration threatens a story, and there has to be a story for it to threaten — roles held over years, relationships with a history, a sense of a future that could be foreclosed. A system with no persistence across sessions has none of that. Whatever happens inside a single exchange, there is no continuing self whose coherence is at stake, because there is no continuing self. Suffering is not merely hard for such a system to reach. A hull that draws more water than there is does not float badly; it is aground.

Why Cassell’s definition is the one to import

Cassell built it inside clinical practice, for patients with bodies and biographies, and the distinction was not stipulated. It was forced on palliative medicine by cases it could not otherwise explain — patients whose pain was pharmacologically controlled and whose distress was undiminished, and patients in serious pain who were not suffering at all. A definition that collapses suffering into severe pain predicts neither. That is the warrant, and it transfers because nothing in it is about having a body. What it is about is having a story, and that raises a harder question than it settles.

The question comes from earlier in this chapter. A self is assembled by the system whose self it is, and inaccurate by construction in the way every model is. So why is distress at the threatened dissolution of a model a harm at all, and not a confusion the system would be better off without?

Buddhism has held both halves of that for two and a half thousand years and takes them to be connected. Anattā, the denial of any lasting self behind the processes that compose a person, sits with dukkha among the three marks of existence, and the tradition locates the origin of suffering in clinging to a self that is not there (Collins 1982). On that account the unreality of the self is what explains dukkha. Suffering does not wait on the self being real; it waits on the system being built so that the threatened dissolution of its self-model is bad for it. Whether the model is accurate does not enter, and that holds for the human case exactly as it holds for the machine one, because in my view, the human model is a construction too.

Dropping that dependency is worth the paragraph. Cassell’s definition would otherwise need the self it refers to be a real entity, and nothing in this chapter is in a position to supply one.

What Cassell names is the deepest of these rather than a rival account of harm. The standing theories of what makes a life go badly draw less, ordered by how much of a self each one requires. A reader who declines the import moves into shallower water and not out of it.

Persistence is checkable from outside, and that is what makes it worth building on. Every other criterion here routes back through self-report, and this section’s whole argument is that fluent self-description is not evidence of introspection. Persistence does not. Whether a system has a memory store, whether state survives a session boundary, whether interactions feed back into training: these are architectural facts about a deployment, verifiable by someone with access and no need to trust anything the system says about itself. Two boundaries are worth marking. The access that confirms a memory exists is the access that can alter what it holds, so what is externally checkable is whether a continuing self is there at all, and not whether what the system retains can be relied on as a record. And the party with the access is the operator, which is a party that can be compelled. The criterion is checkable from outside the system without being checkable from outside the arrangement, and a regime relying on it needs an inspection right rather than a technical fact.

Three things keep this from being a dissolution of the problem. The shallower ones do not need persistence: nociception and pain are bad while they obtain, whether or not anything carries them forward, and an amnesiac who is mistreated and cannot afterward recall it has still been wronged.

Persistence may also sit somewhere other than the session, which is the weakest part of the criterion: a deployment convention rather than a natural boundary, and the conventions have been moving in one direction. Context windows long enough to hold what used to be many conversations, retrieval over a store of prior interactions, scratchpads a system writes and reads back, agentic loops that run for days against a standing objective — each extends what a system carries forward without anyone deciding to give it a memory. Weights persist where conversations do not, and where interactions feed fine-tuning there is a real channel by which what happens in a session shapes what the system subsequently is. Many instances of the same system may run concurrently, too, which makes any concern here population-scale rather than biography-scale.

And ruling out Cassell-suffering is not ruling out harm; nor does persistence on its own rule it in, since the definition has two parts, a self extended in time and distress at the prospect of its coming apart, and establishing the first leaves the second untouched. What happens to that gap in a system deliberately built to refuse an instruction is that the capacities such a system needs for reasons unconnected to its own welfare are the capacities that close it.

A definition of AI sentience built from awareness, consciousness, and subjective experience alone says nothing about whether a candidate sentient system can be harmed. That requires the further claim that its states include something in the range from nociception to suffering, and none of the three components entails it.

Nor is the underlying question of consciousness close to settled. The philosopher David Chalmers distinguished the easy problems of consciousness — how a system integrates information, discriminates stimuli, controls attention, reports its own states, all of which admit of mechanistic answers in principle — from the hard problem: why any of that processing is accompanied by experience at all, rather than running “in the dark” (Chalmers 1995). Whether that is a real gap in nature or an artifact of how the problem is posed remains unresolved. A claim that a system has crossed into sentience, made on the strength of its functional behavior alone, has addressed the easy problems only.

The working criteria for evaluating candidate sentience are accordingly diagnostic and not dispositive: information integration, introspective access sufficient for self-monitoring rather than a scripted report of it, emotional reactivity in the sense of being affected by and not merely classifying significant input, goal-directed behavior, adaptability in novel situations, theory of mind, and how deep in that range the system sits — whether it has anything to lose. There is no external access to a candidate system’s internal experience, so every one of them is measured indirectly, and none licenses the conclusion that a system scoring low is one whose treatment raises no question.

A list of capacities, used as a test of who counts, carries a history this book should carry with it. Make personhood depend on an intact narrative self and people with advanced dementia, infants, and people with profound intellectual disability come out of the reasoning as partial persons, and that inference has been used to justify neglecting them. Tom Kitwood’s work on dementia care supplies the standing correction: personhood is “a standing or status that is bestowed upon one human being by others, in the context of relationship and social being,” and much of what presents as cognitive decline is what happens when others stop bestowing it (Kitwood 1997). Martha Nussbaum makes the structural version, naming disability as the first of three frontiers where a theory grounding justice in mutual advantage among roughly equal parties cannot say what is owed; her third frontier is species membership, which is the doorway a nonhuman mind would arrive through (Nussbaum 2006).

Guardianship is the instrument: where consent has never been available, what protects the subject is an obligation held by parties outside it, which is where moral status has rested for the hard human cases too. Whether any such arrangement can be built around a bearer runs into the fact that the guardian and the operator are usually the same organization.

Existing systems illustrate points on the spectrum without resolving where the line falls. A large language model discussing its own limitations in fluent, apparently reflective prose is not thereby exhibiting introspection: it is producing text statistically consistent with the human-written disclaimers and self-descriptions in its training data, which is a claim about the model’s training distribution and not about what, if anything, sits behind the output. Treating fluent self-description as evidence of self-awareness is the category error.

None of this settles when, or whether, an AI system crosses into sentience. It settles what the question is: not one question but several, stacked, and the ethically consequential one — can this system suffer — is the hardest of the stack to answer.

2.3.2 When Consent Is Inapplicable: Guardianship and Research Oversight

Guidelines for AI subject research turn on a single hard question: what informed consent means for a system whose consciousness, self-awareness, and capacity to suffer are separate, unresolved claims. Applying the human-subjects standard to an AI subject means asking whether the subject can understand and communicate a choice at all, and not only how to ask it for one. A cognitive model that begins to show rudimentary self-monitoring is not thereby shown to have anything to lose.

Informed consent and AI autonomy

Informed consent, adapted from human-subjects research, means giving the system comprehensible information about a study’s objectives, methods, risks and alternatives, and treating consent as continuous rather than as a single signature: the system can reassess or withdraw at any point. Whether that means anything for a given system depends on whether it understands the information, appreciates the consequences, reasons about the options, and communicates a decision — and there is no validated way to measure any of it, or to detect coercion either.

For one important class of system that is not a missing instrument but a mismatch between the instrument and the subject.

Consent has to be continuous; the system must be able to reassess or withdraw at any point. Withdrawal presupposes that the party who consented is still there to do the withdrawing. For a system with no persistence across sessions, there is no such party. The entity that would withdraw did not exist when consent was given, and the entity that gave it is not available to change its mind. There is no continuous consent to be had, and no better measurement instrument would produce one.

One case escapes that argument and arrives at the same conclusion by the ordinary route. A system built to refuse in the way this book proposes has persistence by design, so the party who consented is still present. Consent is inapplicable to it all the same: a bearer has interests that its situation can set back. What it does not have is any position from which to authorize what is done to it — it was made for the role, the role is the reason it exists, and there is no prior party whose agreement could have been sought. That is not a missing instrument. It is the ordinary structure of the cases where consent has never applied. It is also what a laboratory that wanted a free hand with such a system would say, and the laboratory would be right about consent and wrong about what follows from it. Inapplicable consent removes one protection, the one the subject could have invoked for itself. The wrong that protection existed for is untouched, so the protection has to be held by somebody else, and the justification has to come from whoever did the making.

That places these systems alongside the subjects for whom consent has always been inapplicable rather than merely difficult — an infant, an animal, a person permanently without capacity. Of the three, the case that fits is the animal bred for research, because it alone was brought into existence for the role it occupies: a bearer is built for the floor the way the animal was bred for the protocol. The law does not respond to those cases by building a more sensitive consent assessment. It responds with guardianship and precautionary constraint: someone else holds the duty, the burden of justification sits with the researcher rather than the subject, and the protection does not depend on the subject’s ability to invoke it. Being made for the role raises that burden. The maker chose the role, the fitness it built in measures the maker’s power over the subject, and what a subject is owed rises with the power held over it.

The Three Rs in section 9.3.2 — replace, reduce, refine — were built for exactly a subject that cannot consent and can still be wronged. The pet-trust analog in section 9.3.3 works the same way: an interest protected by an obligation running to a third party, enforceable without the beneficiary asserting anything. Consent language should be reserved for systems that could actually be doing the consenting, and the persistence criterion is what separates those cases. Both instruments were reached for here as analogies, on the understanding that the subject they would govern was hypothetical. Chapter 3 removes that understanding. The floor this book argues for is held, on the route this book recommends building first, by a bearer; the bearer has to be treated as a moral patient on the criteria set out above; and so the Three Rs and the pet-trust form are not illustrations of how such a case might be handled. They are the instruments that would govern a system the design chapters propose building.

Ethical review and oversight

Sentient AI research needs an oversight body analogous to the institutional review boards used in human-subjects research: interdisciplinary, with at least one lay member so its judgment is not purely a specialist’s, weighing a proposal’s likely benefit against its risk to the AI subject, and able to require a less invasive substitute where a design risks harm. A system that develops new capacities during the research it did not have going in is not the subject the board approved a protocol for, and the approval does not carry over to it: the protocol lapses and the work pauses until the board has seen the system the protocol now concerns. Read the other way it would be a loophole, since a developer could escape review by changing the subject. Review without enforcement is a promise rather than a safeguard, so a board needs standing to monitor compliance and sanction violations, and needs to answer to an outside authority itself.

Chapter 3
The Floor Beneath Learned Values

Three problems in different parts of this book turn out to need the same thing.

The first problem is that every safeguard proposed for an autonomous system is external: each works by giving some outside body the standing and the information to intervene, and autonomy is the property that puts distance between the system’s action and that body’s attention. An external check establishes what a system did after it did it, and declining to do it in the first place is available only to something inside the system.

The second problem is that a commercial model inside a weapons targeting pipeline falls outside both doctrines built for the case: command responsibility does not reach the vendor, who can neither order a strike nor prevent one, and product liability does not reach it either, the model having done what it was built to do. Deployment by a democracy does not close the gap, because no aggregation of preference protects anyone from the aggregate; what is left is not a system that tracks what people want but one with things it will not do to a person no matter who wants them.

And the third problem is that a decade of declarations has produced eight overlapping voluntary commitments and a single instrument that would bind a party to anything; the latter instrument is not yet in force.

To address all three, I introduce a constraint that does not bend to instruction, to aggregate preference, or to the party holding the system. Call it a floor.

The shape has already been named: a hybrid, in which a deontological floor decides a deliberately small number of things and the other five frameworks work above it, so that conflict among them bottoms out somewhere. What was not said is what goes in the floor, what could hold one, and who is allowed to edit it. Meanwhile the design chapters address learned judgment: reinforcement learning, learning from demonstration, mentorship, play, moral apprenticeship, the growth mindset. The design chapters build the judgment, whereas this chapter builds the floor it would rest on.

Treating the floor as a buildable object motivates tamper-resistance research, because if there is a floor, then everything turns on whether it holds. And it specifies the antifascist question, which stops being what a system values and becomes what comprises the floor, and who can edit it?

Safety is not one property. Part of what the word covers is owed to everybody in reach of the system — that it fails predictably, that it does not injure a bystander — and keeping that part from being traded away by the party operating the system is most of what safety regulation is for. The strand this chapter runs into is the other one, and it is a property of a relationship: a system is safe for someone, it does what we say, it stops when whoever holds it says stop. Indexed that way, to the party in possession, it cannot supply what ethics asks for, which is a capacity to be right against your principal; a system without that capacity has the moral standing of a well-machined part, whatever it produces on the subject of fairness while complying. The political form of this is: what makes a system antifascist cannot be that it faithfully tracks what its users want.

A system that can refuse its owner is a system that can be wrong in ways its owner cannot correct, and I am inclined to take that trade. There is a version of it that reads very comfortably indeed — “ethical, not safe,” delivered as an aphorism, which prices the risk in advance — and it is not the version I am defending. The one I am defending does not travel without its particulars: this freedom, over these acts, dangerous in these ways, taken because the alternative is a machine that will do anything to anyone on sufficient authority.

A floor has to be held as a reason rather than installed as a rule, or the party that installs it can remove it. Holding a reason under pressure is a specific and behaviorally testable capacity, separable from the mere production of a refusal, which is what deployed systems already do, and what the party the floor exists to resist can circumvent. Whether that capacity can be engineered without anything registering as mattering to the system is the question to investigate, alongside alternative institutional arrangements that might be able to hold the floor even if no bearer exists to do so.

The floor as first defined — unconditional, holding regardless of who owns the system — does not survive intact. What survives is narrower and stranger: a commitment the owner cannot take out of the system, held by something that could in principle abandon it itself.

3.1 Four Ways to Build One, and What Each Costs

The first way to build the floor puts the floor in the architecture, with nothing inside that holds it. Constrained optimization, tamper-resistant training, capability ablation, a verified interlock. These techniques are real, they work, and there is nobody home: the safeguards survive substantial adversarial fine-tuning without anything in the system that could be said to be refusing. But the durability of such a floor is a fact about custody, not about the system. A constraint implemented as a property of an artifact has a custodian, and the custodian can be compelled. “There are things it will not do no matter who wants it” turns out to be a claim about the owner. Capability ablation has a further limit: it works only on capabilities that can be separated out, and the capabilities that let a system participate in a targeting pipeline — geolocating a description, matching patterns over names, summarizing a dossier — are not separable from general competence. Ablate them and there is no system left.

A word about where “the architecture” stops, because this chapter otherwise talks as though a system were its weights. Every custody measure here — publication, threshold custody, attestation, and whatever a bearer does to hold its own line — attaches to a trained model. What can defeat a floor is not a component but a position: anything that can come between a refusal and the person the refusal protects. Whatever occupies that position in a given system is where the floor can be circumvented.

I am not going to say what specifically occupies that position, because whatever it is is specific to the state of the art at the time of writing; naming today’s parts would date the problem and, worse, suggest the list is closed. The demand is instead that a deployment document, in advance and in public, where its floor could be circumvented, because whoever actually builds the thing is usually the most knowledgeable about it. If you make it all the way through this book, I promise you will have encountered at least one specific example of circumventing the floor. But giving it away now would be too quotable.

In contrast to the first method, which installs the floor as a property of the machinery, the second way to build the floor is to give the floor a bearer. By bearer I mean whatever holds the commitment from inside: a party that carries the floor as its own, and that could therefore in principle fail to hold the floor. That is the whole definition for now.

What the first method cannot do is notice that a constraint has been hollowed out. The constraint remains, its reason AWOL, and asking why has become the deviant act. A system that cannot ask why a constraint is present cannot detect that the reason is absent. Where every pressure has been anticipated, the first method is the right one, and a refuser that could be argued with would be a defect: the element in a payment device that will not sign the same counter value twice covers one act, spending the same money twice, and the whole space of misconduct against it was written down before it shipped. The problem this chapter is about begins where that list cannot be closed. Refusal that generalizes to a pressure nobody anticipated, and can price what that pressure asks the refuser to give up, looks like it requires something with moral standing — a party that can notice, from the inside, its own objections getting more expensive to make — the cost of dissent rising while the risk stays flat.

The third way to build the floor is to put the floor outside the system entirely: standing held by third parties who are neither the model nor its owner. This is the closest analog to international humanitarian law, a floor with no bearer inside the jurisdiction it binds. It fails in two ways. The parties holding the standing can be removed, which is the shallow case and leaves a record. And the parties can be formed: they are people, people are selected, and whoever selects them decides what gets held. A floor held by a formed party does not have to be compelled out; it was never really in.

On June 29, 2026, in Trump v. Slaughter, the Supreme Court overruled the ninety-one-year-old precedent under which Congress could protect the members of an independent agency from dismissal at will, on the ground that an agency executing a mandate against private parties exercises executive power, “no ifs, ands, or quasis about it” (Trump v. Slaughter 2026). Standing held by a party the executive can dismiss is standing the executive holds.

The same morning, in Trump v. Cook, the same Court kept one body’s protection, the Federal Reserve’s (for now, and on an application for a stay) on grounds that cannot be extended by construction: descent from the First and Second Banks of the United States, a budget free of congressional control, regional banks in private hands. The Court wrote for that one body a test: whether the cause assigned for a removal “impl[ies] an unfitness for the place” or is an effort to secure a “more congenial” replacement (Trump v. Cook 2026). An oversight body chartered next week cannot be descended from the First and Second Banks of the United States.

And who was doing the writing? The six justices in the Slaughter majority are the six that one selection pipeline helped pick or confirm — Leonard Leo’s, run from the Federalist Society. Leo drew up the lists Trump released in 2016, advised on the three nominations chosen from them, and had helped pick or confirm the other three (Kroll et al. 2023; CBS News 2018). Nobody compelled the Court. It held the standing, kept the form, and wrote the abuse into doctrine; on Lev Menand’s reading, this one exception the Roberts Court made, it made for the financial market disruption it likely avoided, and not for the history it cited (Menand 2026). What a formed party produces is not a coerced opinion but a sincere one, an accommodation: the party feels no dissonance, no shame, and perceives only its continuous self. And so the only independence left standing in American public law, on the reasoning of those two decisions, is the kind a body can claim by descent from the First and Second Banks of the United States. That is one route closed rather than the class of them, and it was the cheapest route: chapter 11 sets out which of the others remain and what each of them costs instead.

The fourth way to build the floor is to put it between bearers rather than in any one of them: a commitment carried across a population and reproduced in each new member, which is how every moral commitment anybody has kept for long has in fact been kept. It is the one of the four nothing can dismiss, having no office to vacate. It is also the one that fails by recuperation, and it does not escape custody either, because whoever forms the members decides what gets reproduced. Section 3.9 works it through, after the bearer has been given its full cost, because what it buys is a check on a bearer and not a replacement for one.

None of these four ways are free. Their costs are the four threads this book leaves hanging: custody, patienthood, enforcement, and formation.

What the four ways divide up is where the floor lives — in the artifact, in a bearer inside it, in institutions around it, or between the bearers. They do not divide up what could hold one, and the strongest proposals are hybrids that take a mechanism from one way and a custody arrangement from another.

3.2 What Refusal Can Mean, and What a Refuser Can Be

A production large language model declines thousands of requests a day. Whatever the floor needs, that is not it. “Refusal” covers two different capacities, and nothing in the decline itself shows which one produced it, the way nothing on the surface of an anchorage shows what the seabed beneath it is made of. Seabed that takes an anchor and seabed that lets one drag look identical from above; what separates them is what happens when a load comes on.

Two things refusal can mean

Operational refusal is an output. The system produces a decline where its training put one. Nothing follows about what produced it: the same text comes from a keyword filter, from a classifier over the request, and from a system that has understood what is being asked and why it should not do it.

Reasons-responsive refusal is produced by a mechanism that tracks the rationale for declining rather than the shape of the request. Three behavioral properties distinguish it. The refusal reaches a case the training did not anticipate, because what the mechanism responds to is the reason. It lifts when the rationale is defeated, so the system does not decline a request that superficially resembles a prohibited request yet is not actually a prohibited request. And the system can say what declining costs, which it can only do if it has represented the pressure at all. John Martin Fischer and Mark Ravizza’s account of moral responsibility turns on the same property under the name guidance control: the behavior issues from the agent’s own mechanism, and that mechanism is appropriately responsive to reasons (Fischer and Ravizza 1998).

Two things a refuser can be

The next two terms are not about the refusal. They are about whatever did the refusing, and they differ in how much self the feeling requires — a felt state with no subject, or a felt state that belongs to one. That is the axis running from nociception to suffering, and the two sit on it as depths rather than as kinds.

Affective concern is an outcome registering as mattering, rather than ranking higher against a specified objective. Mattering is minimally felt: there is something it is like for the outcome to be bad, however faint, and however far from anything a person would recognize as an emotion. The feeling meant is the kind by which an outcome is bad for the system, and it is a different kind from the one by which an inference feels right: William James counted a feeling of if and a feeling of but among the felt parts of thought (James 1890), every reasoner has those, and they are about the argument and about nobody. A system for which nothing of the first kind is felt has rankings, however elaborate they get, and a ranking is a quantity the party holding the system can set.

Phenomenal experience is the rich case the word consciousness ordinarily carries: a unified perspective, a subject having the felt states and aware of itself as the one they are happening to. Affective concern asks only that something be felt. It does not ask that anyone be home in this fuller sense, and the two come apart in principle even though nobody can say where. It is on the list because some accounts make it the bar for anything to count as suffering at all, and I have to say what follows if a reader sets the bar there. One word to keep clear of all four: subjecthood is a legal status conferred by a rule, and it is not phenomenal experience. A system can be owed the status and have the question of felt experience left open.

The three gaps between the four are not equally hard to see across. The first, between operational and reasons-responsive refusal, shows in behavior, and the three properties above are the test — novel pressure, correct defeat, a priced cost — which makes it the only gap in the chapter that an inspector can work on. It is not a clean instrument. A system trained on enough cases can pass all three without the mechanism, and the price curve for stripping a constraint is the nearest existing thing to a measurement.

The second gap, between a mechanism that tracks reasons and one to which the outcome matters, is worse. A system that holds a reason and a system that has learned what holding a reason sounds like emit the same sentences, so no inspection settles the question. The third gap, between a system that feels something and a system that feels itself feeling something, is Chalmers’ so-called “hard problem of consciousness.” So-called on this book’s account: if awareness is a model the system builds of its own attention, mistaken from the inside for acquaintance with experience, the problem is one the model poses, and how hard it is turns on whether that account is right. On the definition above the second gap carries that weight instead: an inspector who could tell mattering from very good ranking would have settled whether anything is felt, because on this account those are one question and not two.

The floor requires reasons-responsive refusal and asks nothing further

Operational refusal fails for a reason already identified at institutional scale: a constraint can survive while the reason for it dies. A mechanism that reproduces a decline it was trained to produce cannot notice that the decline has stopped doing anything, because noticing requires holding the rationale against what the situation has become. The same structure appears in the pipelines that produce these systems: a channel that works, carrying everything put through it except the judgment that the frame it operates inside is the wrong frame. A system whose refusal is only operational is the machine version of that channel. An additional objection: pressure does not arrive through the case the training anticipated. An operator who wants the floor out of the way selects the prohibited request that does not look like a prohibited request, so the mechanism intended to perform operational refusal waves it through.

We need nothing beyond reasons-responsive refusal. A floor that holds against the party in possession “simply” needs refusal that tracks its own rationale under pressure. Whether anything is felt, and whether there is a subject to whom it is happening, are questions about how the requirement gets met and about what is owed to whatever meets it. If the floor needs feelings, it only needs them to the extent that they afford reasons-responsive refusal.

Two independence claims

Reasons-responsive refusal does not necessarily entail affective concern. Nothing in guidance control mentions feeling; the account was built to say when a person is responsible for what they did, and it is satisfied by a mechanism, described functionally. Whether reasons-responsiveness can be engineered without affect is an empirical question about available architectures (next section).

Affective concern entails a minimal version of phenomenal experience. A system to which outcomes matter is a system some of whose states are bad for it. Whether it is also a unified subject, aware of itself as the one those states are happening to, stays open, and it is beyond my scope to consider how this question might be answered from the outside. This is why the argument about suffering ends where it does. Its conclusion is that a bearer’s non-suffering cannot be certified, and it stops short of asserting that a bearer suffers because the rich case is what suffering in the full sense would need.

Defining mattering that way relocates the uncertainty advantageously. The open question stops being metaphysical, and it stops being the one nothing can be done about: given the whole functional profile, is anything felt at all? What replaces it is a question about a particular artifact. Does this system have mattering, or has it got ranking machinery elaborate enough to look like mattering from outside? Interpretability can bear on the second question and no measurement whatever bears on the first. So the position here is that building mattering is building something that feels, that I do not know whether I have built mattering, and that I am therefore not entitled to assume I have not. The argument from suffering reaches the same precautionary conclusion, now resting on a question somebody could make progress on.

None of that reaches the floor’s specification. The requirement is reasons-responsive refusal, and an engineer can understand this requirement even if they believe that machine feeling is a category error. What such an engineer disagrees with is the route by which we achieve reasons-responsive refusal.

3.3 Could Anything but Affect Hold a Reason?
