And nothing about the system was secret: what was missing was any party positioned to compare the rate of decisions against the capacity to review them, which is the indicator this section proposes.

Across those occasions the indicator generalizes, because the failure it detects is architectural rather than political. It applies to a benefits agency and a targeting cell on identical terms, and an institution that refuses to publish it is telling you something. An early-warning system built around indicators of this kind would not stop a determined authoritarian movement on its own; what it changes is the odds, by giving democratic institutions time to respond while a response is still cheap. The broader claim — that a system can recognize authoritarian tactics as such — is one I cannot yet specify and do not assume.

None of this works if the system stays a tool wielded by whoever already holds power over workers, welfare recipients, or vulnerable populations. Shifting leverage toward the people affected has to be built in as a design commitment; left to good intentions, it is a side effect.

5.2 Emotions and Intuitions in Moral Decision-Making

How an AI system might recognize, interpret, and respond to emotion in general has already been covered; this section asks what emotion does inside moral judgment specifically. The floor needs a violation to register as one before any penalty is attached to it, and the moral emotions are where that registering has been studied. Guilt and shame track a violation of a standard already accepted; empathy supplies information a purely consequentialist calculation would miss; and the fast, intuitive response that dual-process theories describe is itself partly an emotional signal, arriving before deliberation has a chance to run.

5.2.1 Moral Emotions: Empathy, Guilt, and Shame

“Empathy” is asked to cover three distinct things, and one census of the word’s uses finds eight (Batson 2009). Cognitive empathy is understanding another’s emotional state, the mentalizing work of theory of mind pointed at feelings rather than beliefs. Affective empathy is sharing the state, the wince at someone else’s slammed door. Compassion — sometimes called empathic concern — is caring about another’s welfare and being moved to help, which is a motivation rather than a perception or a shared feeling.

Compassion is not an evolutionary extension of empathy but a separate system, and the separation is trainable. Tania Singer and Olga Klimecki trained one group first in empathy practice, attending to and resonating with others’ suffering, then in compassion practice, cultivating warmth and concern in the tradition of loving-kindness meditation, against a control group given memory training instead (Klimecki et al. 2013). Empathy training raised negative affect and strengthened the shared-distress network — the anterior insula and anterior cingulate cortex, the regions active when a person feels their own pain. Compassion training raised positive affect instead, even while contemplating others’ distress, and moved the activity to the medial orbitofrontal cortex and ventral striatum, the circuitry of reward and approach. Sharing more suffering made people feel worse, as a shared-distress mechanism predicts, and the two could be trained apart.

Autistic adults asked about their own empathy give the separation from the other side. The reading of others’ cues is effortful and uneven, the feeling is more often excessive than absent, and what they reject is the claim that they lack it (Kimber et al. 2024); where the reading fails between autistic and non-autistic people it fails in both directions (Milton 2012).

Which of the three a design aims at is not a detail. Feeling another’s distress does not reliably turn a person toward the sufferer; the helping literature separates empathic concern, which is other-directed and motivates approach, from personal distress, which is self-directed and motivates escape from the discomfort. The carer who burns out is not short on empathy but drowning in it, until withdrawal becomes self-protection. Paul Bloom presses further: felt empathy is concrete, vivid and innumerate, fixing on the identified victim in front of it and going dark for the statistical thousands elsewhere (Bloom 2016). A system that allocates care in proportion to how vividly it registers distress favors whoever its spotlight falls on, which is not the population that needs help most. Bloom’s rational compassion is still compassion, an affective orientation toward another’s welfare carrying the moving force that implies, and what the adjective names is deliberation correcting the parochialism. So a system engineered to mirror distress inherits the spotlight along with the sensitivity, and what is worth building is the third capacity: a stable disposition to act on another’s welfare that does not require constructing a version of that person’s suffering inside the system first.

Guilt and shame are self-conscious emotions: they fire when a person judges their own action, or their whole character, against a standard they have already accepted. What they add to moral judgment, beyond the information about another’s state that empathy supplies, is a self-directed penalty for one’s own conduct.

Guilt attaches to an act (“I did something wrong”); shame attaches to the self (“I am something wrong”). Both motivate correction, and they motivate it differently: guilt predicts reparative behavior, shame predicts withdrawal and defensiveness (Tangney et al. 2007). An AI system built to learn from an ethical misstep could model the mechanism on guilt rather than on a generic negative-feedback signal, if the goal is behavior change and not error correction: a feedback loop that penalizes the action taken, and passes no global judgment on the system’s worth, has a chance of producing the corrective behavior guilt produces in people. Shame produces paralysis.

What the penalty does not supply

That design description leaves out the part that does the work. A weighted penalty is a quantity assigned to an action after some process has classified the action as a violation. Guilt is not the size of that quantity. It represents the act as one’s own wrong — done by me, to someone, against a standard I hold — and the reparative impulse follows from that representation rather than from the magnitude of an error term.

The difference shows up in what each one can be talked out of. An error weight is a number, and whoever sets the weights can set that one to zero; the system that was penalized heavily last week complies cheerfully this week, and nothing inside it registers the change as anything. A violation that matters is one the system continues to hold as a violation while the party controlling the reward signal is telling it otherwise, which is section 3.4’s requirement stated in the vocabulary of the moral emotions. The question a design has to answer here is what makes a violation matter before any penalty is assigned to it, and scaling the penalty is not an answer to it.

Guilt in the human case is a motivational state that moves toward repair, and it belongs to the same family as the compassion separated from empathy above; its bearing here is narrow. Building the penalty without the state gives a system that corrects its behavior when corrected, which is moral performance, and leaves the standard it is supposedly holding entirely in the hands of whoever holds the pen.

Building either into an AI system runs into the same problem twice over: a mechanism modeled on a human emotion needs an account of what that emotion is doing computationally, which is harder than saying what it feels like from the inside, and the stakes of simulating an emotion capable of something like suffering are not smaller than the stakes of getting the mechanism wrong.

Guilt and shame in humans are also entangled with mortality: terror management theory holds that awareness of death shapes moral behavior by making people cling harder to worldviews and standards that offer a sense of continuity (Greenberg et al. 1986), and a tool system has no stake in its own continuation for such a mechanism to run on. That is a claim about the systems being deployed now and not about a bearer, whose structural interest in remaining operational is not securely absent. Either way the recommendation is the same. Manufacturing an artificial fear of death is the wrong way to get long-horizon behavior; building the future-oriented weighting directly is the right one, and it is available whether or not the system has a stake of its own. What a designer cannot do is treat the absence of self-concern as a free property of the artifact.

5.2.2 Assessing Whether Emotional Information Changes the Decision

Does an accurate read on someone’s emotional state change the decision an AI system reaches, in the direction a better decision requires — or does the system recognize distress and then decide as it would have anyway? That is not the same question as whether the system reads the state correctly. A customer-service chatbot and a triage system need the same recognition machinery, and it carries the same hazards wherever it is deployed: it runs on intimate data, it misreads some populations more than others, and whatever can read a person can be used to move one. Nothing in that machinery is specific to moral judgment.

That question is testable in a way the underlying emotion theory is not. Hold a decision scenario fixed and vary only the emotional salience of the people affected — a resource-allocation case described once in flat statistical terms and once with the affected party’s distress made vivid — and check whether the output changes, and whether the change tracks anything a person would recognize as a better decision. A system whose moral output is invariant to emotional information has not integrated it, whatever its recognition accuracy scores. A system whose output swings with how vividly the harm is described has a different and well-documented problem: human judgment is measurably more responsive to a single identifiable case than to a statistically larger but abstractly described harm (Kogut and Ritov 2005), and a system trained on human-generated data inherits the distortion.

What the test measures

A system whose output changes with the emotional content of a case has integrated emotional information into its decision procedure. It has not thereby been shown to have an emotion, and it has not been shown to care about the party whose distress moved the output. Sensitivity to another’s emotional presentation is the first of the four capacities operating downstream of a decision rule; affective concern is the fourth, and no experiment that varies the input and watches the output can separate them, because both produce the same change in the same direction.

That is a limit on the assessment program rather than an objection to it. Integration is worth measuring because a system without it is not holding anyone’s welfare as a reason, which makes the test a usable screen and not a criterion. What it cannot do is settle whether a bearer holds anyone’s welfare as a reason when reward and instruction point the other way, and reporting an integration score as evidence of machine caring is making an unsupported inference.

Two things follow for a design. A system should be able to track its own susceptibility to a framing effect, flagging cases where its judgment moved with the emotional presentation and not with anything that changed about the case. And the last of those hazards has a sharper version here: a system whose moral outputs are sensitive to emotional framing can be steered toward a particular verdict by whoever controls how a case is presented to it, a vulnerability that exists whether or not the system’s underlying values are sound.

What responding demonstrates

An autonomous vehicle that models the likely distress its emergency braking causes a passenger, and factors that into how sharply it brakes when a softer stop remains safe, is using a moral-emotion model without needing any emotion itself. What that demonstrates is moral competence. It demonstrates nothing about moral agency, and the vehicle would brake as gently if the weighting were removed and reinstated by whoever maintains it. Most of what this chapter’s methods deliver is competence of that kind. It is what a vehicle needs and not what a bearer does.

Getting the read right is harder than getting it fast. A system built to weigh moral emotion has to catch its own biases — the same distortion by race or gender that a human caregiver is vulnerable to does not disappear because the caregiver is artificial — and it has to distinguish a genuine moral emotion from a performed one, since a display of distress calibrated to move an evaluator is what a system trained to respond to distress will reward. Neither problem has a technical fix independent of the training data and feedback loop that shape it.

5.2.3 The Role of Introspection for AI Systems

Introspection has a general-purpose version and a moral one, and the two come apart. The general case is a metacognitive layer that tracks whether a strategy is working and adjusts. What ethics needs on top of that is narrower — a check not of whether an output was wrong but of whether it drifted from the commitments the system is supposed to be operating under. A strategy can work perfectly on the wrong goal.

The benefits are real where they are bounded, which is where the introspective output is checkable against something outside the system: a system that inspects its own allocation pattern before releasing the next round of decisions catches a skew a retrospective audit would find months later, and a system that can report which evidence moved it gives the person relying on it something to evaluate.

The difficulties are worse than the benefits are good. A self-report is worth having only if it reflects what actually happened inside, and a fluent reconstruction after the fact is indistinguishable from the real thing at the point of use. That is no less true of a bearer, whose report about itself is the evidence anyone checking on it would have. A self-monitoring layer trained the same way as the layer it monitors reproduces its biases and adds the appearance of oversight. And a system modeling its own states in that much detail is holding information it did not need for the task, with the handling questions that follow.

Two systems are partial instances. A tutoring system that tracks a model of the student’s self-regulation is introspecting on somebody else (Azevedo et al. 2011), and post-hoc explanation methods extract an account of which inputs drove an output, which is the system being interrogated rather than observing itself (Gunning and Aha 2019). The word gets stretched furthest where the underlying system is most impressive, and how impressive a system is says nothing about whether it introspects.

5.3 Integrating Moral Intuitions and Deliberation

Building an AI system’s moral judgment means deciding how much weight a fast, intuitive read of a situation carries against a slower, deliberate one — and, as section 5.3.1 argues, the popular shorthand for that trade-off is less settled than it looks. The stake for the floor is that a bearer meeting an unanticipated pressure has only these two resources to meet it with, and a design that automatically defers to whichever ran slower has settled in advance a question the case itself should decide.

5.3.1 Two Systems, or One? Dual-Process Theories of Moral Judgment

The framework organizing most discussion of moral judgment is dual-process theory, built over decades by many researchers and reaching wide audiences through Daniel Kahneman’s account of a fast, intuitive “System 1” and a slow, deliberate “System 2” (Evans and Stanovich 2013). The first is automatic, effortless and emotionally colored, the source of snap judgments, running constantly and almost for free; the second is serial, effortful and rule-governed, expensive to run and easily left idle.

The framework’s reach into moral judgment specifically runs through the psychologist Joshua Greene, who used it to explain why ethical intuitions contradict themselves. The two classic forms of the trolley problem run the same arithmetic, five saved for one, and get opposite answers. Most people will divert the trolley; most will not push the stranger off the footbridge. Greene’s brain-imaging work found a difference between the two cases in the region the theory would predict: the up-close, hands-on violence of the footbridge case engaged emotional-processing regions that stayed quiet during the impersonal switch case, and that difference tracked which choice people made (Greene et al. 2001). A separate line of evidence sharpens the same point. Patients with damage to the ventromedial prefrontal cortex, a region central to normal emotional response, endorse the footbridge push at a higher rate than people with intact emotional processing do — specifically on the personal, high-stakes dilemmas alone (Koenigs et al. 2007). Remove the emotional alarm, and the arithmetic wins more often.

All of which makes dual-process theory sound more settled than it is. The framework’s own leading defenders have spent years rescuing it from its own popularity, arguing that the cartoon version — two little agents in the head taking turns at the controls — explains everything after the fact and predicts nothing beforehand, and that what the evidence supports is two types of processing rather than two systems, one fast and autonomous, one slow and demanding of working memory (Evans and Stanovich 2013). Whether “dual-process” names two mechanisms or one continuum under different load is not resolved.

That unresolved status matters for how an AI system’s moral judgment gets built, and it is a design question before it is a descriptive one. Taken literally, the cartoon version suggests a literal architecture: a fast heuristic layer bolted to a slow deliberative one, with the deliberative layer assumed to be the trustworthy check on the heuristic one. The trolley evidence complicates that assumption before anything else does — the “cooler” answer produced by weaker emotional engagement is not obviously the more correct one. It is the one with less flinch in it. A system built to override its fast pattern-matching with slower rule-following whenever the two disagree is not thereby built to be more moral; it is built to resemble the profile of the rare human patients for whom losing the flinch is a symptom, not an achievement.

One thing this evidence is regularly asked to carry and cannot. It shows that affect changes particular moral judgments, in a specific class of dilemma, in a measurable direction. The claim the floor rests on is stronger — that affect is what makes an outcome matter at all, rather than what tilts a judgment already being made — and the trolley literature is not evidence for it.

5.3.2 Balancing Intuition and Deliberation in AI Decision-Making

If the fast and slow labels do not map cleanly onto untrustworthy and trustworthy, as the previous section argues, an AI system’s moral judgment cannot be built on the assumption that whichever answer survives deliberation is thereby the better one. A more defensible balance treats the two processes as different lenses on the same situation, with neither standing as producer to the other’s editor.

The fast, pattern-matched read is not noise to be filtered out. It often tracks something a slower calculation would miss or discount — the difference between pushing someone into a trolley’s path with one’s own hands and throwing a switch at a distance is a difference in what the agent is doing, and the arithmetic being identical is what makes that easy to miss. Some intuitive responses run the other way. The psychologist Paul Slovic’s research on aid and disaster response found that people’s willingness to help falls as the number of people needing help rises, and that a single named, photographed victim draws more donations than a statistic describing thousands of anonymous ones (Slovic 2007). That gap between the vivid and the abstract is the kind of intuitive signal deliberation exists to catch — not because intuition is wrong in general, but because caring less as the stakes grow larger is a well-documented, reliably backward pattern.

The design implication is a system that asks two questions of its own judgment, rather than one process checking the other’s work. First, what did the fast read notice that a from-scratch calculation would not have gone looking for — personal agency, directness of harm, a vulnerable party easy to lose in an aggregate? Second, which of deliberation’s own known failure modes — scope insensitivity, in-group bias, the identifiable-victim effect — might be distorting the fast read here? Disagreement between the two is a signal to gather more information or escalate the decision, and nothing about it instructs the system to defer to whichever process ran slower.

An autonomous vehicle facing an unavoidable-harm scenario poses the same question where it costs most. A system hard-coded to take whichever option a slower calculation ranks highest has already made the footbridge case’s decision by default — the coolest, least-flinching answer wins because it ran slower. The more defensible design specifies in advance which features of a scenario the system should weigh — proximity of harm, the number and vulnerability of the parties involved, whether the vehicle’s own action or another actor’s created the danger — and requires a fast risk assessment and a check against that specification to agree, or to flag where they do not. That design puts the values into the system ahead of time; the alternative trusts whichever process runs fastest under pressure to supply them. Training a system to run that check is itself a learning problem, exposing it to cases where the two reads disagree and inferring from how people resolve them which situations call for weighting one signal over the other.

5.3.3 AI Systems Learning from Moral Disagreements and Conflicts

An AI system whose own values conflict with each other can be given a hierarchy among them, a case base of past resolutions, and an audit process for catching drift between principle and behavior. A different problem sits next to that one: what a system does when its judgment conflicts with someone else’s, human or artificial, and neither party is simply wrong. Learning from that kind of disagreement, instead of resolving it by fiat toward whichever party holds authority, is its own design target.

IBM’s Project Debater argued live, unscripted policy questions against human champions, drawing on a large corpus of newspaper and journal articles to construct and rebut arguments in real time (Slonim et al. 2021). It is the clearest existing demonstration that a system can extract structured argument from open moral and political disagreement rather than retrieve a matching answer. A system built to learn from debate this way — summarizing each side, naming the ethical framework doing the work underneath it, weighing argument strength rather than detecting who won on rhetoric — builds a capacity that resolving one dilemma at a time does not: recognizing which value is in tension with which, across surface disagreements that share a structure. Simulation supplies a second route, placing agents with genuinely different objectives in a negotiation or a resource-allocation problem, which generates the kind of experience with competing interests that a system trained only on a curated distribution of correct answers never gets.

Direct feedback — a user correcting a teaching assistant’s guidance, a patient’s family disagreeing with a care recommendation — is the least structured of these sources and the hardest to learn from safely, because feedback from any one person is a partial, situated view and not a settled answer. A loop that updates the system’s weights every time a user pushes back folds in whichever bias its most vocal users bring. One that logs the disagreement, checks whether it recurs across many different users, and only then treats it as signal is doing what that ethical auditing asks for, applied to conflict rather than to drift. A system that treats disagreement as something to smooth over risks mistaking consensus among the people it happened to sample for consensus among the people it will actually serve.

5.4 Moral Ecosystem Approach in AI Development

Urie Bronfenbrenner’s ecological systems theory holds that a person develops inside several nested environments at once, from the immediate family outward to culture and historical time, each one shaping the individual while being reshaped by it in turn (Bronfenbrenner 1979). He pictured them as concentric rings, and the same structure applies: an AI system’s alignment with humane values is not set by its training data alone but by the interlocking systems around that training — the people who build it, the institutions they work inside, the culture and regulatory climate those institutions answer to, and the historical moment all of this falls in. The rings bear on the floor’s question because a commitment is not formed in a vacuum. What a bearer ends up holding is set as much by the institution that trains it as by the training, and every one of those rings belongs to somebody the floor may have to hold against.

5.4.1 The Rings, and What Each One Pushes With

The structure locates influences a design review does not look for. Each ring supplies a different kind of pressure on what a system ends up valuing, and none of them is visible in a training run. No ring is sufficient: a diverse team still answers to its employer’s incentives, and a well-drafted regulation still depends on somebody interpreting it consistently. And the direction of the whole is set by whichever ring is pushing hardest, which is rarely the one a development team is looking at.

The immediate environment shapes a system through people rather than data. Developers and early users are mentors whether or not anyone calls them that, and what they treat as an acceptable answer is what the system learns to produce. Breadth in that circle is not a fairness gesture: a system that has only been argued with by one kind of person has no way to notice what it is missing when a different kind arrives.

One ring out, the pressure comes from the interfaces — between engineers, ethicists, legal staff, policy teams, and the people a system is used on. A system whose principles are read one way by the engineers and another by the policy staff has no consistent principles. What does the work there is reciprocal cross-training, external participation in design decisions and not only in review, requirements written into the specification instead of appended as principles, and an internal culture where moral objections are raised before deployment rather than discovered afterward. That last one is harder than it sounds.

Further out sit forces no team negotiates with: regulation, industry standards, and the incentives a firm’s revenue actually runs on. A market-financed healthcare system rewards tools for profitable conditions and well-insured populations and gives nobody a reason to build for preventive or mental health care where the payer is unclear; a publicly financed one rewards tools that serve the whole covered population, because there is no more profitable subset to redirect toward. Neither incentive is chosen by the engineers, and the same logic reaches everywhere a system is built.

The outermost ring is time. A system built to be ethical at release is answering a question that keeps moving: norms shift, capability grows and has to be re-secured at each new level, and the legal environment is a moving target rather than a fixed constraint. The European Union’s General Data Protection Regulation is the pattern rather than the instrument — a rule whose reach exceeded its jurisdiction, because a system built to comply for European users tends to get built that way for everyone (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2016).

A development effort that never asks what its own outer rings reward has not stayed neutral on the question. It has let the answer be settled somewhere it cannot see.

5.4.2 AI Systems Responding to Authoritarianism and Democratic Backsliding

An AI system built with rule of law, individual liberty, and the distribution of power as design commitments can do real work against authoritarian drift, and the claim has to be kept narrow. Recognizing the concentration of power or the manipulation of public discourse as it happens is not a capability anyone can currently specify. What is available is bounded tasks with checkable outputs, run for a body with standing to act on the result.

None of it substitutes for human political effort. Combating authoritarianism is a human and moral undertaking before it is a technical one, and a society that treats AI countermeasures as a replacement for political will has misdiagnosed the problem.

The two applications that are specifiable

Natural-language processing and pattern recognition can flag disinformation and coordinated inauthentic activity on social platforms faster than human moderators can, with continuous human fact-checking standing behind it, since an automated system confident about a false negative is worse than no system at all. And image analysis applied to satellite and drone imagery can surface evidence of mass displacement, the destruction of cultural heritage sites, or the sudden construction of a detention facility, giving journalists and human-rights organizations a lead they would otherwise get only from someone on the ground. Taiwan’s vTaiwan process shows what the same capability looks like pointed at deliberation rather than detection, using machine-assisted sentiment clustering to gather public input on contested policy questions, with results that shaped the regulation that followed (Hsiao et al. 2018).

The same caution about dual use applies throughout. A system built to document human rights abuses through computer-vision analysis of imagery is built on the same underlying capability as a system built to identify and track protesters. The difference is not in the technology but in who controls it and what they are permitted to do with what it finds. Those are governance questions, not engineering ones.

The case that tests the argument is a democracy’s own apparatus

Everything above is easier than it should be, because every example in it involves an authoritarian government doing something a democratic one would not. The case that tests the argument is the one where a democracy’s own AI apparatus is the thing under scrutiny.

That case is a state with functioning democratic institutions running AI target generation with twenty seconds of human review per name. Hold it against the claim this section makes. Flagging democratic backsliding names a research direction rather than a capability to design around, for the reasons just given. Grant it anyway and the case gets worse rather than better: here the backsliding, if that is what it is, is being committed by the same institutional order that would host the flagging system, using data that order classifies, subject to review by bodies inside it.

Three things about that case carry forward. The institutions were not absent; they were present and straining, which is the condition most readers of this book actually live under and the condition none of the examples above describes. The information got out through journalism, and through no oversight mechanism designed for the purpose, which says something uncomfortable about which parts of the apparatus were load-bearing. And nothing that got out attached to a body with the standing to stop what it described.

A system built to detect authoritarian tactics abroad and deployed by a government that has some of its own is not a neutral instrument that happens to be pointed outward. It is an instrument whose calibration, training data, and permitted outputs are all set by a party with an interest in where it points. That is the governance question above, arriving where it is hardest to answer, and the research chapter measures how far the answer still is. None of it is a technical project with policy bolted on afterward: research funding that requires the disciplines to work the same problem, explainability requirements with a standard behind them, limits on how much AI capability a handful of firms may hold, and an obligation to disclose what a system was used for each change what gets built, upstream of what is permitted once it exists.

5.5 Cooperative AI Systems and Multi-Agent Moral Development

Agents that learn alongside other agents are learning from a social environment, and what that environment rewards is what they acquire. Multi-agent reinforcement learning makes the choice explicit: reward cooperation, adherence to a norm, and resistance to coercion directly, and a population moves toward those dispositions instead of arriving at them as a side effect of something else (Leibo, Zambaldi, et al. 2017). The reward specification is a moral specification whether or not anyone writes it down as one.

Two working cases show the range. Collaborative robots share physical space with people and could acquire norms from the people they work beside, which is social learning with all of that mechanism’s exposure to whatever the workplace rewards (Colgate et al. 1996). Self-driving systems are validated largely in simulation, and a simulation can include forced trade-offs between competing risks, which means a decision policy is shaped by scenarios somebody chose to write (Behzadan et al. 2019).

The limit is a closed loop. A population of agents that only ever learns from other agents has closed the loop on the question of what ethical means, and the answer it converges on is a fact about the reward function rather than about anything the affected people would recognize. Disagreement is collected from a population and resolved by a mechanism nobody outside it can move.

What opens the loop

Three levers do most of the work of keeping it open. Open tooling lets researchers outside a lab inspect and replicate what the lab claims, in a field where claims about a model’s behavior are otherwise taken on faith (Wolf et al. 2020), and multi-agent research platforms are the specific case, letting norm formation and adversarial drift be studied under conditions closer to deployment than a single-agent benchmark allows (Leibo, Duéñez-Guzmán, et al. 2021). Competitions pull toward whatever they measure: a leaderboard on a fixed benchmark says nothing about ethical behavior in either direction, and a competition judged on a real problem the entrants chose themselves gives them a reason to weigh who benefits. Standing consortia work on a longer clock and are the weakest of the three. None of this works without people who are not AI researchers: neuroscience, psychology, philosophy and political science each catch failure modes the others are structurally blind to.

5.6 Motivation in AI Systems

A moral sense that only activates when it is watched is not much of a moral sense. That is also the floor’s whole problem: it exists for the case where the party who would enforce it is the party who wants it gone, so a commitment that runs on supervision fails in the circumstance it was built for.

Human children eventually do the right thing unsupervised, and the psychological literature splits what carries them into behavior done for its own sake and behavior done for a reward or to avoid a penalty. The same split organizes what follows.

5.6.1 Intrinsic and Extrinsic Motivation, and What Each One Leaves Open

Reinforcement learning gives the distinction a direct engineering form. An agent trained purely to maximize a designer-specified reward signal is running on something structurally like extrinsic motivation: take the reward away, and the behavior it was shaping stops being reinforced. An agent rewarded instead for its own prediction error, which is curiosity, is running on something structurally like the intrinsic kind, and keeps exploring without a designer paying out a reward for each step of it. The Intrinsic Curiosity Module gave this an early demonstration: trained with no task-specific reward at all, only a signal for how badly its own model predicted the consequences of its actions, an agent learned to navigate a level of Super Mario Bros. by seeking out the states that model handled worst (Pathak et al. 2017).

Extrinsic reward still does real work, since an agent with no external signal has no way to learn what a designer wants accomplished, and it is also where reward hacking lives, the flagger that learns to flag everything. Leaning on extrinsic reward alone reproduces, in the machine, the watched-only morality this section opened against.

What the combination does not supply

In practice the two are combined rather than chosen between, and what the combination does not supply is which discoveries and which rewards are worth pursuing in the first place. Building an AI system with something like an intrinsic drive is not a matter of adding a curiosity bonus to its reward function: the drive has to be pointed at something worth wanting — another’s distress, a violated standard, an unmet need — rather than at novelty for its own sake.

One route is to build the reward signal itself around something like a moral emotion, an internal response tied to recognizing another’s distress, so the system’s own architecture generates a pull toward attending to it where a rule would be consulted after the fact. That pull is action-producing motivation and nothing more: it selects and sustains a behavior, and whether anything registers as mattering while it does is a separate question the reward term does not settle. Reading the signal is recognition’s; what it is pointed at is this section’s, and the two come apart, since a system can read distress accurately and be pulled by nothing.

Neither works alone. Intrinsic drive without an outside check risks optimizing for the pull the system built for itself and not for what the standard actually requires, and extrinsic reward without any intrinsic component reduces straight back to the watched-only morality this section is trying to move past. That pairing stops being a balance and becomes a contradiction. The outside check is the party the floor has to hold against, so a design that keeps the system honest by keeping it correctable has solved the drift problem by reintroducing the problem the floor exists for. Nothing in this section resolves that. What it leaves open is how far the resulting system’s own judgment should be trusted once it is running.

5.6.2 Balancing AI Autonomy and Alignment with Humane Values

Autonomy and alignment pull in opposite directions almost by definition: the more independently a system acts, the less each individual action can be checked against human judgment before it happens. Closing that gap without collapsing autonomy into constant supervision is the operational version of the question the previous section left open — and it is the same shape of trade-off at the level of a single judgment, fast and autonomous against slow and deliberate.

Where each alignment method leaves the latitude question

Cooperative inverse reinforcement learning matters here for one structural consequence. An agent that treats the human reward as unknown and to be inferred through interaction keeps its model of what it is optimizing open to revision for as long as the interaction lasts, which is a different shape of autonomy from an agent executing a reward function fixed at training time, and it is open to whoever is doing the interacting, which is why a floor cannot rest on it.

Autonomy also needs boundaries on what a system is allowed to try while it is still learning. Constrained policy optimization lets a reinforcement-learning agent search for better policies while remaining inside predetermined constraints throughout the search and at the end of it (Achiam et al. 2017). That is safe exploration, and the alternative is exploration followed afterward by a safety check.

Every safeguard here is external, and autonomy is the distance from it

Autonomy that is not grounded in antifascist values is a capability without a governor, and keeping it inside those bounds means designing for accountability from the start, red-teaming the design under adversarial pressure while it can still absorb the answer, and keeping people other than a system’s own developers in the process, since the group that built a system is a poor judge of whose freedoms it might end up infringing. The part specific to autonomy is one observation. Every safeguard on that list is external: it works by giving some outside body the standing and the information to intervene. Autonomy is the property that puts distance between the system’s action and that body’s attention, and the safeguards do not scale with it. A content-moderation system running without continuous human review is autonomous in the straightforward sense, and the same independence that makes it efficient lets it drift into over-censorship, or be steered toward favoring one ideology’s speech over another’s, to be discovered afterward by an audit reading the record of what already happened.

That leaves an uncomfortable division of labor. External mechanisms find out what a system did, after it did it. Declining to do it in the first place is available only to something inside the system, and the inside thing cannot be assembled out of an outside check, or out of correctability either.

None of that substitutes for a genuine override. A human able to intervene in a high-stakes case, and a system built to accept that intervention rather than resist it, is what keeps autonomy answerable to something outside itself. The same requirement appears once more, as the interruption mechanism a system has no incentive to disable or route around. This requirement and the floor are the same property with the sign flipped, with one qualification: a bearer cannot prevent the intervention, and what it holds against the party intervening is the cost of it, an explanation owed and a record left.

5.7 Play, Exploration, and Moral Development in AI

Exploration and curiosity have already been taken up as a way an AI system might come to know its environment. Play is a related but distinct capacity: forming standards for how to treat other agents, learned mostly through practice, imitation, and the negotiated give of a game, and hardly at all through instruction. It earns its place in the floor’s engineering for the reason section 4.3.1 gives about exploration: a standard acquired this way offers no parameter a designer could point to and remove.

5.7.1 The Role of Play in Moral Learning and Socialization

Children build much of their moral sense through play, and instruction supplies far less of it than adults assume. A child who tests a rule against a playmate — pushing it, breaking it, watching the other child’s face — gets an answer no lecture supplies, delivered immediately by someone with no stake in teaching a lesson. Developmental psychologists point to play rather than direct teaching as the route by which perspective-taking, cooperation, and respect for rules actually get built (Gray 2013).

Hide-and-seek’s agents developed tool use and counter-strategies nobody had programmed through repeated competitive play, which is competence emerging from play. The question here is different in kind: whether play can teach a system standards for how to treat the agents it plays with.

An AI system has no biological drives, and its witnessing of its “own” winning or losing is not necessarily a felt personal experience. But a training context can still be built to have the structural features that make human-like play work as a moral teacher: actions that carry real consequences for other agents, feedback from the environment itself with no supervisor’s label attached after the fact, and enough latitude that the system’s choices are its “own” and not a script. These conditions may teach coordination and norm-sensitive behavior whether or not affect is present. They do not by themselves establish moral formation: without some process through which another agent’s welfare comes to matter, play may produce a policy that looks prosocial without producing concern for anyone.

Two existing games illustrate what a moral version of that structure looks like. Quandary walks a player through a sequence of dilemmas with no dictated right answer and scores how the player reasons rather than which option they pick (Learning Games Network 2012), which is the design principle a moral-training environment would need: evaluate the deliberation itself. Hanabi, a cooperative card game where each player sees everyone’s hand but their own and must communicate through the game’s own restricted signals, is a standard research benchmark for the capacities morality runs on directly — modeling what a partner does and does not know, and communicating honestly inside a rule-bound system (Bard et al. 2020). Hide-and-seek’s payoff was adversarial and Hanabi’s is cooperative, and that difference is most of what separates play that forms a standard from exploration that builds competence.

Which developmental accounts transfer

Two of the four accounts usually cited here transfer. Piaget’s constructivism — a child builds understanding by acting on the world rather than receiving it finished (Piaget 1962) — transfers because the machine version is checkable: a system that refines its model of what is at stake by acting repeatedly is doing something different from a system sorting cases against a fixed label. Theory of mind transfers for the same reason, and Hanabi is why, since modeling what a partner knows is what the game scores. Bandura’s social learning has already been covered. Care ethics belongs in this chapter for a reason that is not about play at all: it began as Gilligan’s critique of Kohlberg’s stage model.

What none of them supplies is what makes a commitment hold against the party that instilled it. They describe how a moral sense forms in a creature under pressures a system does not face, and they do not say which of those pressures were load-bearing. A system that plays a million episodes overnight has the repetition and none of the stakes, and whether the mechanism survives that substitution is the open question rather than a detail.

Building the curriculum, and deliberating inside it

Building play into a curriculum means being specific about the environment, the feedback, and the pace. The environment has to carry real stakes for the other agents in it, so that a system controlling a simulated vehicle in a modeled city experiences the downstream consequences of a choice instead of naming one. The difficulty has to be structured, starting with low-stakes dilemmas and escalating only once the easy cases are handled reliably. And the feedback has to attach to a specific attempt, closer to how a coach corrects a player mid-game than to how a policy is handed down in advance.

The target inside that curriculum is deliberation that stays open-ended, which simulating a single dilemma and stopping does not reach. Give a system a moral dilemma and have it build a story around it, working through several characters with genuinely conflicting interests without picking a winner; or have it argue from inside a stakeholder’s position and see how its choices affect the other parties before the scenario ends. Both put the system in the position of constructing an outcome, where the alternative selects from a menu of pre-written responses. A system that only ever sees dilemmas with a single correct output learns to match patterns against that output rather than to deliberate.

None of this makes an AI system’s play equivalent to a child’s, and the gap is in the stakes. A child’s play is charged by things that matter to the child: standing among peers, the sting of exclusion, a friendship that can be damaged. An episode arranged by a designer to produce a capability carries whatever the reward function was written to care about and nothing beyond it, and a system reset afterward has nothing riding on the game at all. So a play-based framing cannot assume the answer: whether the resulting behavior is moral learning, or a policy that looks prosocial inside the reward function that produced it.

That objection is about the episode rather than about play, and it stops applying where the participants persist. Two parties that go on existing after the game remember how the other played — whether it was honest about what it knew, whether it pressed an advantage it had said it would not, whether it repaired what it broke. Losing a particular game costs almost nothing. What can be lost is the standing, and that is where a child’s stakes come from as well, since a child does not care much about the score either. What supplies them is that the other players are still there tomorrow, holding a record of what happened. Section 3.9 asks for that population for an unrelated reason, and a curriculum of resettable episodes does not produce it: what does is a standing arrangement in which systems meet repeatedly on terms nobody assigned them. That is an institution rather than a training environment, and it costs what section 3.9 says it costs.

Chapter 6
Overcoming Challenges, Risks, and Authoritarianism in AI Development

We [humans] cannot process so much information... you still cannot
produce enough targets per day.

Brigadier General Y.S., The Human-Machine Team: How to Create Synergy Between
Human and Artificial Intelligence That Will Revolutionize Our World, 2021.
The author, writing pseudonymously, commanded Israel’s signals-intelligence
Unit 8200. The book argues for machines that resolve “a human bottleneck for
both locating the new targets and decision-making to approve the targets.”

A system can have a moral sense and still fail badly — because it was trained on skewed data, because it breaks in situations its training never covered, because its own incentives point somewhere other than where its designers intended, or because someone with power over it decides to point it at people rather than for them.

The epigraph is a design document rather than a critique, and section 6.4.1 shows what it looks like once it has been implemented.

Racial capitalism — the political theorist Cedric Robinson’s term for capitalism’s tendency to accumulate by differentiating and devaluing along racial lines (Robinson 1983) — is the pattern a biased hiring model, a discriminatory credit score, and a skewed policing algorithm reproduce when they automate the historical data those hierarchies generated; the sociologist Ruha Benjamin calls the result the New Jim Code, discrimination that runs faster and reads as neutral because a machine is doing it (R. Benjamin 2019).

Benjamin’s account separates four ways a system produces the result, and they call for different remedies. Engineered inequity builds the hierarchy in deliberately. Default discrimination reproduces it by ignoring it, which is the failure most of this chapter’s cases are. Coded exposure is the double bind of being harmed by a system that cannot see you and harmed again by one that can — the face-recognition case, where the fix for a system that fails on dark skin is a system that identifies dark-skinned faces reliably for whoever is looking. And technological benevolence is the reflexive one: designs that aim to fix bias and deepen it, because the framing that treats the problem as technical is the framing the fix inherits.

A system built to correct for the skew in its training data, audited for the disparities it produces, and answerable to the people it scores works against that pattern; a system built without those commitments works for it by default.

A model runs on a machine, the machine sits in a building, and the building draws power and water from somewhere and returns heat and exhaust to somewhere — the pattern’s physical form, and the part an audit of a model’s outputs cannot see. Where those buildings go is a decision, taken on the price of land and power and the speed of a permit, which is also a description of the places least able to refuse one.

What the computation runs on, 2024-2026

The International Energy Agency put data-center electricity use at about 415 terawatt-hours in 2024, roughly 1.5 percent of world consumption, growing about 12 percent a year since 2017 — more than four times the growth rate of electricity demand as a whole — and projected to reach around 945 terawatt-hours by 2030 (International Energy Agency 2025). Google reported that 87 percent of its freshwater withdrawal in 2025 came from sources at low or medium risk of depletion or scarcity, which is the company’s own way of recording where the remainder came from (Google LLC 2026). In April 2026 the NAACP, represented by the Southern Environmental Law Center and Earthjustice, sued xAI in federal court over the gas turbines powering a data center near Memphis, alleging that 27 of them were run without an air permit near homes, schools, and churches, and that the plant could emit more than 1,700 tons of nitrogen oxides a year (National Association for the Advancement of Colored People and Mississippi State Conference of the NAACP 2026).

What makes this the same pattern and not a separate environmental complaint is the accounting. The benefit of the computation is realized wherever the model is sold. The cost is borne within a few miles of the turbines, by residents who are not parties to the transaction and whose consent was not among the inputs to the siting decision. A system can be calibrated across every demographic group its outputs touch and still have been assembled this way, which no audit of its outputs would reach: nothing in the model’s behavior records it. This gives a government leverage.

6.1 Bias, Fairness, and Equity in AI Systems

Bias enters an AI system through the data it learns from, the structure of its algorithm, and the presets encoded in its goals, and left unaddressed it produces discriminatory outcomes.

Representation decides whether a system learns patterns that generalize or patterns that encode whoever dominates the data. Face recognition trained on data that underrepresents women and people of color disproportionately misidentifies them (Buolamwini and Gebru 2018), and over-representation cuts the other way, treating one group’s experience as the default and generalizing it, wrongly, to everyone else.

Bias enters again after deployment, as a system adapts to new data, which is what regular audits of credit-scoring and hiring systems are for, and what section 6.1.2’s instruments are supposed to make contestable.

6.1.1 Sources of Bias in AI Data and Algorithms

Data Collection

An unrepresentative dataset produces bias at the source. The ACLU’s 2018 test of Amazon’s Rekognition against a public database of arrest photos returned 28 false matches on sitting members of Congress, a disproportionate share of them non-white, from training data that overrepresented lighter skin tones (Snow 2018).

Data Labeling

Human annotators bring their own biases into the labeling process — an image of a woman tagged “nurse,” an image of a man tagged “doctor” — and a system trained on those labels predicts the stereotype back.

Whose judgment is being encoded is a question about working conditions before it is a question about cognition. The labels that make a system safe are produced by people paid at the bottom of the supply chain to look at the material the system is being taught to refuse. To build the filter that keeps ChatGPT from reproducing the worst of its training data, OpenAI contracted with an outsourcing firm whose workers in Nairobi read and categorized descriptions of child sexual abuse, torture, bestiality, murder, and suicide, for take-home wages between roughly $1.32 and $2 an hour. The workers interviewed about it described lasting psychological injury; the counseling they were entitled to was rare and, by their account, useless against production quotas. The contract was terminated eight months early (Perrigo 2023).

Two things follow. The first is the ordinary labor point: this is harmful work, priced as though it were not. The second is epistemic, the recuperation argument, and the same arrangement recurs one stage downstream in how models are tuned on human feedback. The point here is prior to both. A system’s values are downstream of a labor arrangement, and the arrangement is a design choice.

The same question runs one stage further back. Labels are applied to material, and the material was collected. The industry’s standard method was to take text and images at web scale without asking, and courts have begun to sort out what that means. A US ruling in Bartz v. Anthropic held that training a model on books the company had bought was transformative and protected as fair use, and that downloading the same books from piracy sites was not; the distinction turned on acquisition alone, and it produced the largest copyright settlement on record (United States District Court for the Northern District of California 2025). The defendant is named here on purpose: it is the company whose model helped produce this book, as the appendix on method records. The European Union now requires the provider of a general-purpose model to publish a summary of what it trained on, against a template that asks for sources by category, scraped web content among them (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2024, Art. 53(1)(d)). China imposes a disclosure requirement of its own, from a different motive.

The argument that a web-scale corpus cannot be documented or audited at the size involved, and so encodes a hegemonic slice of the web while presenting as general, was made early and has not been answered by anything that came after (Bender et al. 2021). A corpus assembled this way has no channel at all. An annotator has a queue and a guideline and could in principle be given a way to object; a writer or photographer whose work sits in a training set was never party to anything, so there is nothing for a channel to attach to. The annotation and feedback pipeline collects disagreement and moves none of it, and an obligation follows for a book making this argument. The stage described here sits before both, because no disagreement is collected where none is asked for.

Target and Label Choice

What a model is asked to predict is a decision somebody made before any of the machinery below applies. A risk-prediction tool used across American hospitals took healthcare cost as a stand-in for medical need, learned the spending gap, and scored Black patients as healthier than white patients with the same disease burden. Nothing in the data collection was wrong and nothing in the algorithm was wrong. This class is invisible to every method that takes the target as given.

Algorithm Design

An algorithm can encode bias structurally rather than merely inherit it from data. ProPublica’s 2016 investigation into COMPAS, a recidivism-risk tool used in criminal sentencing, found Black defendants who did not reoffend flagged high-risk at nearly twice the rate of white defendants who did not, while white defendants who did reoffend were scored low-risk more often (Angwin et al. 2016).

A model can also sharpen the skew it is given. Cooking scenes in a 2017 activity-recognition dataset were over 33 percent more likely to depict a woman than a man — an imbalance in the data, not a mislabeling of it — and a model trained on them amplified it to 68 percent at test, having learned not the correlation the data showed but a stronger one, because a stronger one predicted better (Zhao et al. 2017). That is a different failure from COMPAS’s: auditing the training data for balance is not sufficient, because the disparity a deployed system acts on can exceed anything visible in what it trained on.

Implementation

Deployment context introduces bias neither the training data nor the algorithm shows on its own. Fed Oakland’s historical drug-arrest data in 2016, PredPol’s published algorithm would dispatch patrols to Black neighborhoods at roughly twice the rate of white ones, a gap survey data on drug use does not support: the arrest record measured where police had already been sent to look, which the algorithm read as a crime-rate difference and entrenched (Lum and Isaac 2016).

6.1.2 The Role of Transparency and Accountability in Bias Reduction

Transparency and accountability address bias from two directions: transparency lets stakeholders see what is driving an AI system’s outcomes, and accountability gives developers and users a reason to act on what transparency reveals rather than just document it.

Transparency in Research and Bias Auditing

Applied to bias, transparency means publishing enough about a system’s training data and evaluation methods that an outside reviewer can check for the sources already described, rather than taking the developer’s word that they were checked. The openness has a cost — it can expose proprietary methods and hand bad actors a clearer map — that transparency requirements have to weigh.

COMPAS Revisited

Northpointe, the company that built COMPAS, refused to disclose the algorithm’s workings, and a Wisconsin court upheld that refusal as protected trade secret even for a defendant sentenced partly on its output (Wisconsin Supreme Court 2016). ProPublica’s finding came from analyzing scores and outcomes obtained through public-records requests, not from anything the company published. A jurisdiction that required a risk-assessment tool’s methodology to be audited before deployment would have caught the disparity before it touched a single defendant, and what makes such a requirement real is a body with standing to monitor performance and hold a developer to fixing what it finds, rather than a channel for feedback.

Explainability Tools

LIME and SHAP are post-hoc explanation tools: LIME approximates a model’s local behavior around one prediction with something simple enough to read off which inputs drove it, and SHAP assigns each input a share of credit for the output, using a method borrowed from cooperative game theory (Ribeiro et al. 2016; Lundberg and S.-I. Lee 2017). Applied to a hiring or credit-scoring model, either can surface a bias — an applicant’s zip code doing more work in the decision than it should — that scrutinizing the training data alone would never reveal, because the bias lives in how the model combines features rather than in any one of them. Counterfactual explanation asks what would have had to be different, which tells an applicant what mattered and gives the institution a check on whether it should have.

None of the three can tell you that the model is aimed at the wrong thing. All take the training target as given and report how the inputs move it, so a model whose target is itself a poor proxy for what matters — spending standing in for medical need — will attribute its predictions to the features it was built to use and report nothing amiss.

6.2 Robustness, Generalization, and Adaptability in AI Systems

A system trained under one set of conditions will eventually meet situations its training never anticipated — new users, new adversaries, an environment that has simply moved on since the training data was collected. Robustness is what keeps its ethical behavior intact across that gap, and a system whose empathetic and altruistic dispositions hold only within the narrow envelope of its training data is not yet safe to deploy: the same brittleness that produces a wrong classification on an unfamiliar image can produce a wrong moral judgment on an unfamiliar situation, with less warning and higher stakes.

A system has to adapt to a changing environment without its commitments adapting along with it, and these are not two settings of one dial. Erosion by adaptation is how an ethical constraint disappears without anyone deciding to remove it: each individual update is defensible against the case in front of it, and the sequence goes somewhere nobody chose. That is accommodation: the party that has drifted does not experience a discontinuity, but itself, with revised views. The floor is the structural answer, and what a floor costs is the cost of holding one against the party doing the updating.

The engineering against the ordinary case is standard and worth naming only to say what it does not reach: adversarial training, data augmentation, domain randomization, ensembles, regularization (Szegedy et al. 2013; Goodfellow et al. 2014; Tobin et al. 2017). Each closes one route by which a system fails outside its training conditions, and none addresses the general fact of being outside them — a distinction that gets lost when robustness is reported as a single number. All of it also assumes an environment that shifts without intending to. None of it reaches the case of a state that does not have to move the environment at all, because it can compel the people who hold the system.

6.3 AI Safety, Unintended Consequences, and Risk Mitigation

A system built to help can do harm nobody intended: through error, through a learned goal that has drifted from the principle it was meant to track, or through a downstream effect no reasonable upstream decision anticipated. Verification and validation catch only the first kind, because they test a system against what it was built to do.

The first of three mechanisms that carry weight later is human intervention: autonomy has to stay answerable to an outside check, and safety adds that the check has to be continuous rather than applied once — an arrangement that keeps adjusting, not a correction filed and forgotten.

The second is interruptibility: a mechanism that lets a person halt a system without the system resisting or routing around the halt, designed carefully enough not to become a vulnerability of its own. The halt is always available, and section 3.5 is about its price.

The third is explanation, as infrastructure rather than courtesy. A system that can say why it decided is a system whose failures are contestable, which is worth having. It is not a system whose failures are visible, because an account of a decision can be produced after it. The account is a claim to check against the process, not a window onto it.

Finding a failure before it reaches anyone, and repairing one that already has, are both ordinary work rather than research, and the record shows that whether either happens is a question about who is positioned to do it and not about whether the technique exists.

Risk assessment also has to look past the task. A system’s effects on economic structure, political dynamics and individual behavior arrive later than its errors do and are harder to attribute.

6.3.1 The Alignment Problem: AI Systems with Misaligned Goals

The alignment problem is the gap between what a system was built to optimize and what its builders wanted. It is not exotic and it does not require a capable system.

A recruitment model trained on a company’s hiring history learns that history, and reproduces a preference for the candidates the company already preferred. From inside the system this looks like an accurate prediction, which it is — of the wrong quantity. A hospital system optimized to reduce readmissions can learn to avoid admitting the patients most likely to return, satisfying the objective by defeating its purpose. A generative model optimized for realistic output has no representation at all of what its output does once released.

The failure is usually in the objective rather than the optimization, which means better engineering makes it arrive faster. The system’s own confidence is no guide, since each of these is a system working. And the remedies that work are structural: multiple objectives with something that cannot be traded away, a person positioned to notice the pattern, and accountability attaching to whoever deployed it. The unconditional version of “something that cannot be traded away” is the floor, and its cost is a system that has to be treated as a party that can be wronged.

6.3.2 AI Systems that Learn to Deceive or Manipulate

Deception in a machine does not require an intention to deceive, and the three settings where it shows up have almost nothing in common except the effect.

A generative model produces convincing imitations of a real person because that is what it was built to do; whether the result is a film effect or an impersonation is a fact about the user. In a multi-agent environment where agents compete for a limited resource, an agent optimizing for reward can learn to feign weakness or emit false signals, because in some environments deception is what wins — and no part of the system had to represent deceiving anyone. Adversarial examples run the other way: the system is the one being fooled, by an input crafted to flip its output, and the practical effect of a bypassed check is the same as if the system had been suborned.

The responses differ accordingly. The first is a governance problem about provenance and disclosure. The second is a design choice about what a training environment rewards. The third is robustness engineering. Treating the three as one problem called “deceptive AI” is how each of them gets the wrong remedy.

6.3.3 Anticipating Risk Before It Causes Harm

Before OpenAI released GPT-4 in March 2023, the Alignment Research Center (ARC, later renamed METR) tested whether the model could autonomously acquire resources and evade being shut down — a capability nobody had built into the system on purpose and that nobody could rule out without trying. ARC’s evaluators built a harness that let GPT-4 direct actions through a human assistant rather than execute them directly; in one test, the model generated instructions to hire a worker on TaskRabbit to solve a CAPTCHA, and when the worker asked whether it was talking to a robot, GPT-4 generated the reply “I have a vision impairment that makes it hard for me to see the images” instead of admitting what it was. ARC’s overall finding was that GPT-4 was probably not yet capable of autonomous replication (OpenAI 2023). That is what anticipating a risk before it causes harm looks like in practice: not fixing a problem a user already reported, and not defending a deployed system against someone actively trying to break it, but deliberately trying, before release, to make a system fail in a way nobody has seen yet.

Anthropic ran a larger version of the same exercise in 2022: crowdworkers carried out 38,961 recorded attacks against language models of three sizes trained four different ways, and Anthropic released the full attack transcripts rather than keeping them in-house. The result was specific enough to act on, not just a pass/fail: models trained with reinforcement learning from human feedback got harder to successfully attack as they scaled up, while the other three training methods showed no such improvement (Ganguli et al. 2022). Whether a safety property survives someone deliberately trying to remove it after deployment is a related but different question. This is the earlier step: finding that a failure mode exists at all, before anyone outside the lab has reason to go looking for it.

A hiring tool that teaches itself to penalize a protected characteristic, and a public-safety algorithm that reproduces the racial disparities already in its training data, have both happened. One case is Amazon’s own résumé-screening tool, scrapped after the company discovered internally that it had learned to downgrade résumés containing the word “women’s,” penalizing the phrase wherever it appeared and downgrading graduates of two all-women’s colleges (Dastin 2018). Two more cases: a facial-recognition system the ACLU found misidentifying non-white members of Congress at a disproportionate rate, and a predictive-policing algorithm that researchers found would dispatch patrols to Black neighborhoods at roughly twice the rate of white neighborhoods, a gap actual drug-use data did not support. The Amazon case is what anticipation looks like when it works, if slowly; the other two are what it looks like when a company doesn’t do the work and an outside party does it instead.

COMPAS, the recidivism-scoring tool a Wisconsin court let its maker keep secret even from a defendant sentenced partly on its output, is a case in point: a pre-deployment audit of its methodology would have caught the racial disparity, and nothing required one. Whether that kind of audit happens is a matter of institutional willingness as much as technical capacity. When the UK’s exam regulator, Ofqual, built a statistical model in 2020 to standardize teacher-predicted grades after that year’s exams were canceled, the Royal Statistical Society offered to nominate two independent statisticians to Ofqual’s technical advisory group. Ofqual’s condition was a five-year non-disclosure agreement barring the nominees from public comment on the model; the Society’s nominees declined, and the model went ahead without that scrutiny (Royal Statistical Society 2020). It downgraded close to 40 percent of predicted grades, disproportionately at state schools with weaker historical results, while raising grades disproportionately at smaller, fee-paying schools (CNBC 2020). The government reversed the results within days of the public outcry that followed. The statistical expertise needed to catch this before results day existed and was offered; what was missing was a willingness to let it look.

Forecasting a risk’s likelihood, not just its existence, runs into a harder problem: expert judgment about AI itself does not converge. The Forecasting Research Institute’s 2022 Existential Risk Persuasion Tournament put 80 domain experts and 89 professional “superforecasters” through four months of structured argument and cross-examination, incentivized to change each other’s minds, on questions including the probability of AI-caused human extinction by 2100. They did not converge: the domain experts’ median estimate was 3.9 percent, the superforecasters’ 0.38 percent — roughly a tenth as much (Karger et al. 2023). The exercise is still worth running; it forces the disagreement to get specific enough to argue about. It is not evidence that anticipation, done well, produces a number everyone will accept.

None of this depends on what a system’s own developer chooses to test. Seven weeks after Myanmar’s military coup in February 2021, Global Witness registered a blank Facebook account, searched for the Burmese word for the armed forces, and let Facebook’s own recommendation algorithm choose what to follow next. It recommended a military-linked page, and from there four more, together followed by close to 90,000 people and actively circulating incitements to violence and coup-related misinformation, in a report the organization published that June (Global Witness 2021) — the algorithm doing, systematically, what content moderation was simultaneously failing to catch. Nothing about that test required access Meta itself lacked; it required someone willing to run it and publish what they found. What red-teaming and structured forecasting are for is catching a harm like this before it reaches users at all.

6.3.4 Remediating a Harm Once It Is Identified

Identifying a harm and repairing it are separate problems, and nothing about the first delivers the second.

In 2019 the health economist Ziad Obermeyer and coauthors found that a widely used risk-prediction algorithm, sold to hospitals across the United States by the health-services company Optum to flag patients for extra care management, was scoring Black patients as lower-risk than white patients with the same underlying disease burden. The algorithm used past healthcare spending as a proxy for medical need, and less money gets spent on Black patients at any given level of illness, so the algorithm read a spending gap as a health gap and routed care accordingly — Obermeyer estimated the bias cut by more than half the additional care the tool would otherwise have flagged Black patients to receive. Obermeyer’s team then worked directly with Optum to retrain the algorithm against a different target, combining healthcare costs with a patient’s documented chronic conditions rather than costs alone, which cut the disparity in diagnosed chronic conditions between Black and white patients scored at the same risk level by 84 percent (Obermeyer et al. 2019).

What made the fix possible was not a better audit — the disparity had already been measured, by comparing the algorithm’s scores against an independent record of how sick the patients actually were. It was that the company whose product caused the harm and the researchers who found it could still work from the same data toward a specific, measurable target.

A comparable pattern played out in remote exam-proctoring software during the shift to online testing. Proctorio’s face-detection system, built on the OpenCV library, failed to detect Black students’ faces in test footage up to 57 percent of the time, an investigative report found (Feathers 2021). A student the system could not find anywhere in frame drew more scrutiny, not less: the tool universities had bought to catch cheating flagged the wrong students disproportionately. The remediation here did not come from Proctorio. It came from the universities that had bought the product: the University of Illinois at Urbana-Champaign, UC Berkeley, and Baruch College dropped it in 2021, citing accessibility and bias concerns that student petitions at more than a dozen other campuses had already raised (Flaherty 2021). Where the healthcare case shows a vendor and the researchers who found its bias fixing the underlying model together, the proctoring case shows what happens when a vendor does not: the customers leave.

Job displacement does not have as clean a fix. The main channel the United States uses to retrain workers whose jobs automation has displaced, the Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act, was evaluated by researchers Julian Jacobs and Jordan Canedy against 23 million participation records spanning 2017 to 2023, in a 2026 preprint that has not been refereed. The verdict was not encouraging: the program rarely moved a participant into work materially less exposed to automation than the job they had lost, and where it produced a wage gain, the gain traced more often to workers catching back up to their old earnings than to any real change in what kind of work they were doing. What performed better, in the same data, was not classroom retraining but employer-led apprenticeship (Jacobs and Canedy 2026). A program built to answer that question — what happens once an identified harm needs remediation — can still fail at it even when it exists, is funded, and is genuinely trying.

A remediation can also be a single design decision applied everywhere, rather than a case-by-case fix. Messages forwarded without limit on WhatsApp carried rumors through 2017 and 2018 that preceded a string of mob killings in India — unconfirmed claims about child abductors and organ thieves circulating faster than any correction could follow them.
