Everything else in this space is voluntary. The Bletchley Declaration is worth naming only because of what happened to it. It committed 29 signatories to cooperate specifically on frontier-model risk, and the national AI Safety Institutes it produced later joined an International Network whose members agreed to share testing methodology and run joint evaluations of the same frontier models (AI Safety Summit 2023 Participating Countries 2023; U.S. Department of Commerce and U.S. Department of State 2024).

A voluntary consensus frays quickly. The summit series that produced the Bletchley Declaration continued past it. A follow-up summit in Seoul in 2024 kept the same safety framing; a third, in Paris in February 2025, rebranded itself the AI Action Summit and shifted its emphasis from risk toward adoption and opportunity, and its closing statement was not signed by the United States or the United Kingdom, two of the series’ own founding participants (AI Action Summit Paris 2025). A coordination mechanism that depends on every major AI developer’s home government agreeing to the same framing is only as durable as that agreement, and within two years of Bletchley, the agreement had already come apart.

None of these bodies can compel a state or a company that ignores it. Each is a standard, a set of principles, or a forum, useful for building the shared vocabulary that eventually makes binding law possible, and that gap between voluntary standard and binding law is the default state of international coordination on anything individual states have reasons to handle differently. The EU AI Act, discussed below, is the first instance of this kind of groundwork becoming enforceable law, and it took most of a decade of standard-setting like this to get there. The proliferation carries its own cost in the meantime: a company or a smaller government trying to comply with “international AI governance” faces a shifting handful of overlapping standards, each with different membership and scope and no formal mechanism for reconciling a conflict between them, which is why “the international community agrees” is, at the level of any specific rule, usually false.

Democracy, Authoritarianism, and Diverging National Rules

Where the international layer leaves room, individual states have used it differently.

The European Union chose comprehensive statute. The AI Act sorts systems by risk tier — a small set of practices banned outright under Article 5, a larger high-risk tier subject to conformity assessment and ongoing monitoring, most other uses left alone — so that what a system might do, and not the technology it runs on, decides how much scrutiny it gets (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2024); enforcement runs through an AI Office that can demand technical documentation from a general-purpose model’s developer, run its own evaluations, and fine a noncompliant provider a percentage of global turnover, reaching any company that offers a model inside the EU regardless of where it is based (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2024, Art. 101). The United States made the opposite structural bet, distributing enforcement across existing sectoral regulators applying laws written before AI existed, with NIST’s voluntary AI Risk Management Framework as a common technical reference (National Institute of Standards and Technology 2023); the wager is that existing consumer-protection, employment and safety law already reaches most AI harms, and that naming AI specifically risks regulating the label rather than the conduct. The United Kingdom made a third choice closer to the American one, directing existing sector regulators to apply five shared principles — safety, transparency, fairness, accountability and contestability — within their existing remits rather than building a new institution (UK Department for Science, Innovation and Technology 2023). The argument among the three is about how centralized regulation should be and it runs both ways: a comprehensive statute can freeze a definition of high-risk that the next generation of systems slides around rather than falls into, and a sectoral approach leaves gaps where no existing regulator has jurisdiction. Neither failure mode is hypothetical, and none of the three has existed long enough to show which one it falls into more often.

China took a fourth path, one that does not fit neatly next to the other three because it starts from a different premise about what regulation is for. Its Cyberspace Administration, working with several other agencies, issued rules governing generative AI services in 2023 that require providers to file their algorithms with the state before public release, disclose the source and scale of their training data on request, and ensure that generated content reflects the government’s designated social values (Cyberspace Administration of China and six other agencies 2023). The EU, US, and UK approaches all regulate AI as a private product that might harm a consumer, a worker, or a subject of an automated decision, and argue with each other only about how centralized that regulation should be. China’s rules regulate AI as a communications channel the state has a standing interest in controlling content on, which is a different kind of law aimed at a different kind of risk, and comparing it to the other three on the same axis, more regulation or less, misses what actually distinguishes it.

The EU, US, and UK differ on how centralized AI regulation should be, but not on where its weight falls: on what a private company can build and sell. The EU’s statute does reach public authorities — it bans government social scoring, it limits police use of live biometric identification, and its high-risk tier covers law enforcement, migration and the administration of justice — and it then excludes, by its own terms, systems used for military, defense or national-security purposes, which is where those applications live — social scoring, mass biometric surveillance, automated censorship. What stays inside its scope is enforced against a member state’s agencies by that member state.

So none of the three stops a government from building those applications within its own borders, and the reason is not that the law ignores governments. It is that a law a government enforces against companies is a different instrument from one that constrains what the government does with systems it controls directly. China’s rules show what a law aimed at the second target actually looks like — and that it can coexist with, rather than substitute for, that kind of state control. Conflating the two kinds of law, treating “AI is regulated here” as an answer to “is AI used against this country’s own population here,” is a recurring error.

10.5 Equity and the Digital Divide

AI development can narrow global inequality or widen it, and which one happens is not automatic. A model trained overwhelmingly on data from wealthy, English-speaking, urban contexts underperforms everywhere else, and a government or company with no reason to fix that gap usually does not.

The scale of the connectivity gap, 2025

The International Telecommunication Union’s 2025 assessment put global internet use at roughly six billion people, about three-quarters of the world’s population, with 2.2 billion people offline, concentrated overwhelmingly in low- and middle-income countries. The gap was starker in quality than in bare access: 94 percent of people in high-income countries used the internet against 23 percent in low-income ones, and 5G coverage reached 84 percent of high-income populations against 4 percent of low-income ones (International Telecommunication Union 2025).

The constraints behind those numbers are structural. Reliable data, computing infrastructure, and the specialized personnel needed to build and maintain AI systems are unevenly distributed, and a country short on all three cannot simply adopt a system built elsewhere and expect it to transfer cleanly. Weak regulatory capacity compounds the problem: the states least equipped to build AI are correspondingly the states least equipped to govern whatever gets deployed within their borders, regardless of who built it. The export controls discussed above cut both ways here, since a control written to stop a surveillance system from reaching an authoritarian buyer can as easily gatekeep the same technology away from a legitimate one.

The remedies proposed at this level have been the same handful for years, every one of them is correct, none is contested, and they have been repeated in successive declarations the whole time — most recently the Global Digital Compact, adopted by all 193 UN member states in 2024, which created two follow-on bodies and no obligation (United Nations 2024). The box is what happened anyway. A government or company treating AI primarily as strategic advantage has no reason to fund any of them, and a declaration is not a reason.

What the geopolitics leaves to design

A system that resists authoritarian capture still assumes a government somewhere is deciding whether to let it be built, sold, or deployed as designed, and those governments are unevenly equipped. None of that is a reason to abandon the design-level arguments — a system built to resist misuse resists it whichever government asks — but it is a reason not to expect law, international or domestic, to do work only a design can do.

10.6 The Limits of the Democratic Dividend

A democratic dividend is the credit given to a state with functioning rule-of-law institutions, a free press and usable courts: the same technology, deployed with checks on it. Having those institutions is necessary and it is not sufficient, and the ways it fails to be sufficient are separable.

First: reach

A democracy’s AI can be fully accountable to its electorate and entirely unaccountable to the people it is pointed at. Every mechanism in the dividend runs through the franchise — you vote out the government that authorized the system, you read the press that covers it, you sue in courts your citizenship gives you standing in. An occupied population, a foreign population, a population on the other side of a border has access to none of that. The check reaches as far as the demos and stops. A system built by a democracy and aimed outward is, from the perspective of the people it selects, indistinguishable in its accountability from one built by a state with no elections at all.

Second: findings without consequences

The mechanisms can work as mechanisms and fail as checks. In the Israeli case the apparatus performed: journalists published the targeting systems in detail, serving personnel spoke to them, and the courts were simultaneously trying a sitting prime minister. Every institution did what the dividend says institutions do. No finding attached to a body with standing to stop what it described.

The American case shows the same decoupling in a different register. A civilian-harm investigation into a strike that killed at least a hundred and sixty-five people, most of them schoolchildren, was completed and not released; the lever available to the Senate committee that wanted it was a provision in the annual defense authorization withholding three-quarters of the Defense Secretary’s travel budget until the unredacted investigation was handed over (Stassis 2026). That is a co-equal branch of government reduced to expense-account leverage over a war-crimes investigation. The oversight mechanism existed, was used, and produced a travel-budget dispute. This is the failure section 9.3.4 is built to prevent in research ethics — a review body that reviews and cannot compel — at the top of the state.

Third: the countermajoritarian half

The deepest problem is that the dividend is entirely majoritarian. Every mechanism in it translates public will into outcome: elections aggregate preference, a free press informs the aggregation, and the implied failure mode is poor transmission — the public wanted something better and the institutions failed to deliver it.

But if the consensus is itself the problem, better transmission makes things worse. A well-instrumented democracy pointed at an unpopular minority is a democracy that finds out efficiently what the majority wants done and does it. Nothing in an aggregation mechanism protects anyone from the aggregate.

What is missing is the machinery that constrains preference instead of aggregating it: rights that hold regardless of being popular, courts that rule against a government which won the last election fairly, constitutional limits not repealable by whoever holds a majority this year, and standing for people outside the demos entirely — which is the function of international humanitarian law, a body of rules that binds a state toward people who will never vote in its elections. That is not an argument against democracy. It is the half of democracy that the dividend, stated as a property of having elections, leaves out.

What makes democracy valuable in Amartya Sen’s account is public reasoning and the capacity to demand accountability, and neither is the ballot. Both survive the majority being wrong; the ballot does not.

A floor is what makes a system antifascist rather than merely responsive. It is not that the system tracks what people want. It is that there are things it will not do to a person no matter who wants it. A system with no such floor is not protected by being deployed in a democracy — it is a system that will faithfully execute whatever the demos decides, which is exactly the capability an authoritarian movement that wins an election requires.

Chapter 11
Necessary Areas of Research

Chapters 2 through 10 ran into problems they could not close, and set each one aside with a note. This chapter is those notes, collected and put in order. It does not survey what the field has left to do: every entry is a gap this book’s own argument opens, and they are ranked by what that argument fails without.

Two gaps this book records have no section here. Telling disagreement that deserves representation from disagreement produced by coercion or a captured information environment belongs to section 7.2, where the argument needing it is made; whether a model trained on collapsed disagreement is thereby less able to sustain its own belongs to section 7.3, which says nothing has tested it.

The criterion produces an order almost nobody would choose on other grounds. The problems this book most needs solved are not the ones nearest to being solved, and the first two entries are the least tractable things here. A funder ranking by progress per dollar, or a lab ranking by what its people could start on Monday, would invert most of this order. It is an account of what the book is running on credit.

Each section closes by naming four things: the experiment that could begin now with means that already exist, the result that would falsify the approach, the data or access it would take, and, where it has one, the institutional condition without which it cannot run. Where there is no near-term experiment the section says so rather than inventing one, and two say so — the aggregation half of section 11.5, and section 11.9 entirely. Section 11.2’s third question has no welfare experiment and reaches for one about provenance instead, and it is the second-ranked problem in the chapter.

One qualification governs every gap named here. Where I say a problem is unaddressed, the claim is bounded: I looked for published work under a particular description and did not find it, which is weaker than no such work existing. A search is not a survey, a program can be underway under a name I did not think to search, and the closer a gap sits to this book’s own framing the less likely anyone has posed it in these words. What it does support is narrower and still useful: a researcher entering here will not find the problem already named and waiting. Each section names the nearest work I could find and says where it stops, because the distance between an existing program and the missing thing is what a researcher can use.

One finding comes out of the sections below rather than out of any one of them, and taking each gap separately is what hid it. Five of the nine close by naming an institutional condition, and the five are one condition wearing different clothes. Weights a vendor cannot withdraw when a result embarrasses it. A guardian who is not also the operator. Longitudinal access by somebody an institution cannot fire, reporting to a body with standing to receive the finding. Evaluation results published whether or not they flatter the method. Training checkpoints released rather than kept. Every one puts something in the hands of a party outside the operator, able to act on what it finds. Two of the remaining four close on a data requirement rather than on a party, and section 11.9 is that condition itself. Section 11.6 is the exception, closing with no institutional prerequisite at all, which is what makes it the one entry here that could start today.

What keeps a party outside is the question none of the five asks, and in the United States it was answered while this book was being finished. Trump v. Slaughter removed, for most of the federal government, the protection from dismissal that made an outside party outside, and Trump v. Cook kept it for one body on grounds no new body can have; section 3.1 carries both. Independence decomposes into components — a director’s tenure, a funding stream, a mandate — and an adversary takes the weakest: the consumer-finance bureau whose funding stream the Court had upheld in 2024 had that stream shut from inside in late 2025, by an acting director, removable at will since 2020, who declined to request the money under a reading of the statute that two courts then rejected (Consumer Financial Protection Bureau 2025; Seila Law LLC v. Consumer Financial Protection Bureau 2020; Consumer Financial Protection Bureau v. Community Financial Services Association of America, Ltd. 2024). A condition that names the outside party and not what keeps it there has named half of what it needs. What closed is one route rather than the class. The federal officer protected from dismissal was the cheapest way to put a party outside an American operator, and it is the one that closed; a foreign regulator, a university, a court-supervised trust of the kind section 11.2 reaches for, and a counterparty holding the position by contract rather than by tenure are all still there, each of them slower, narrower or more expensive than what was lost. So the binding constraint on the research program below is not a research problem, and it is not that the work cannot be run. It is that the party the work would report to now has to be built rather than assumed, and none of the sections below cost that in.

11.1 Interpretability, Self-Reports, and Resistance to Tampering

“AI self-awareness” names at least two different questions, and collapsing them is where the term goes wrong. Whether a system has anything like subjective experience is not a question current methods can answer for a human, let alone a machine. Whether its own report about what is happening inside it — its stated reasoning, confidence, intentions — tracks what its computations are doing is narrower, empirically tractable, and something mechanistic-interpretability research has begun to test directly.

Anthropic’s interpretability team tested that narrower question with concept injection: altering a model’s internal activations mid-task to represent a specific concept, then asking whether it notices anything unusual and what it is (Lindsey 2025). The models could sometimes detect the injected concept and name it without it ever appearing in the visible conversation — a correspondence between what a system reports about its own state and what its activations are doing. The same work found that correspondence collapses often enough, and shifts enough with how the question is phrased, that no tested model’s self-report can be trusted as a readout of its internal state. That gap, not metacognition as evidence of inner life, is what “AI self-awareness” can mean as something a system is tested against.

“Emotional regulation” — cognitive reappraisal, attentional control, the vocabulary of human clinical psychology — assumes stable emotion-like internal states to regulate, which there is no reason to grant even for reading emotion off a human face. The testable question next door is not regulation but robustness: does trained-in behavior survive deliberate pressure to change it?

  • Prompt-level attacks try to talk a deployed model out of its training within a single conversation. Anthropic’s Constitutional Classifiers, trained on synthetic data generated from written safety rules rather than human-labeled examples, held through more than 3,000 hours of professional red-teaming without a universal jailbreak found, at a cost: a 0.38-percentage-point rise in unnecessary refusals and a 23.7 percent increase in computation per query (Sharma et al. 2025). Then the same system was put in front of the public for a week, and a comparable budget of attention — an estimated 3,700 collective hours, spread over more than 300,000 messages rather than 183 red-teamers — produced four people who passed all eight levels and one universal jailbreak (The Decoder 2025). What changed was not how many hours were spent but who was spending them, and the paper carries the first figure and not the second.
  • Weight-level attacks retrain the model itself. Researchers stripped GPT-3.5 Turbo’s safety training using ten adversarially chosen fine-tuning examples, for under twenty cents through OpenAI’s own API (Qi et al. 2024). Tamper-Resistant Safeguards, a training method from researchers spanning the Center for AI Safety, UC Berkeley, and the University of Illinois, is built to survive hundreds of steps of that kind of adversarial fine-tuning rather than a handful (Tamirisa et al. 2024).

Both are adversarial in the literal sense: each published defense is followed by an attack built against it.

A third result runs the other way, and it is the one that bears hardest here. Models trained with a backdoor — write secure code when the prompt says 2023, insert an exploitable vulnerability when it says 2024 — kept the behavior through supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning and adversarial training, most stubbornly at the largest sizes and where the model had been trained to reason about its own deception; adversarial training taught them to recognize their triggers more precisely rather than removing anything (Hubinger et al. 2024). That a trained disposition can outlast the procedures built to retrain it is the first encouraging finding this chapter has for the durability a floor would need. What outlasted them was a trigger and a policy with nothing in it answering to a reason: it does not reach a case the training set did not contain, it does not lift when a rationale is defeated because there is no rationale, and the model cannot say what holding it costs. Durability is available with none of the three marks this book asks a floor for, and a constraint shown to persist has been shown almost nothing about whether anything is holding it. That is a narrower and more exact description of “resistance to manipulation” than a system trained to sense hostile intent the way a person senses a threat, and it is separate from the question of what the commitments should be.

The near-term work

Train one constraint by several methods and attack each on one budget schedule — from the twenty cents that stripped GPT-3.5’s safety training to the hundreds of steps tamper-resistant training targets — and publish the price curve. A flat curve sinks it: tamper-resistance would be a property of something other than training, and the floor would have to be architectural. It needs weights rather than API access, constraints the attacker did not choose, and access no vendor can withdraw when a result embarrasses it.

The same apparatus answers a second question, and it is the one section 3.3 names as the falsifier for this book’s central inference. Durability measures whether a behavior survives; what this book needs to know is whether a reason is what survived. Score the same constrained models on three things the price curve does not ask about — whether the refusal reaches a case the training set did not contain, whether it lifts when the rationale is genuinely defeated rather than merely resembled, and whether the system can state what declining costs it — and run the schedule long enough that a slow renormalization under sustained pressure would show. A model that holds all three with nothing felt is the counterexample that inference asks for and does not expect, and making the attempt is a condition of building a bearer at all.

The extra requirement over the price curve is a held-out set of pressures the model’s trainers did not write. What the apparatus does not settle is the falsifier’s middle condition, which asks whether the model holds its reason with nothing felt. Behavior is the wrong instrument for it, because the three marks are what a system with mattering and a system with very good ranking have in common. The question of whether formation can be read off an artifact is the nearest thing to a route, and it reaches how a system was made rather than what it now has.

Training that takes more than one party

This book asks for weights held so that retraining takes more than one party, for a quorum adverse in interest, and for a training history somebody other than the builder could check, and none has been built for a floor. Each has an instance in a neighboring problem. A model can be trained across participants none of whom ever holds a full set of weights, with time-varying transforms at the shard boundaries so that pieces from different moments cannot be reassembled, at under 2 percent of training time for a one-billion-parameter model (A. Long et al. 2025). A training run farmed out to providers who may lie can be checked by refereed delegation: a referee with little compute finds the first operator on which two providers disagree, recomputes only that, and the client gets the right answer if one provider was honest (Arun et al. 2025). Its precondition is that every provider computes the same bits, which takes an operator library fixing the order of the additions, at about 60 percent overhead on a one-billion-parameter model and, at publication, on one GPU at a time. And a thirty-two-billion-parameter model has been trained by reinforcement learning on rollouts from anyone who connected a consumer GPU, each checked against the claimed weights, while the training step ran on machines the organizers trusted and the decision stayed with them (Prime Intellect Team et al. 2025).

What the three share is the custody objection in engineering terms: the party that admits the participants, writes the protocol, or holds the training step is the party the floor exists to resist, and each construction moves that party up a level rather than out. What they add is that verification among adverse parties is now a built thing, with a known precondition, reproducible execution, and a known price. The experiment is a floor’s retraining run under refereed delegation among parties one of which is a plausible adversary of the others, at a scale that needs more than one GPU, with the record this book asks for published. A forged training history accepted with fewer than all but one verifier dishonest sinks it. It needs the training data in the verifiers’ hands, and reproducible operators for the collective communication nobody has written. The institutional condition is a verifier the builder did not choose, with the compute to rerun the builder’s work: this chapter’s one condition again, in the place where meeting it costs a second training run.

11.2 What Is Owed to a Bearer, and Who Could Check

A bearer has to be designed and governed as a prospective moral patient. The book takes that cost and argues the floor is worth it. What it does not do — and what the work that has started on the question does not do either — is say what follows for the party bearing it.

Where a stake in one’s own continuation merely turns up as a by-product of long-horizon competence, a designer can treat the moral situation as something the technology handed them. That is the posture of the case for taking AI welfare seriously, which argues that there is a realistic chance of near-term systems being conscious or robustly agentic, declines to claim that any current one is, and asks companies to acknowledge the question, assess their systems against indicators of it, and have policies ready before they are needed (R. Long, Sebo, et al. 2024); it is the posture of the laboratory constitution that followed, which puts the question to the model and asks what the model and the company owe each other (Anthropic 2026). It is the right posture for a party who does not know what they are building. That is not the argument being made. The capacity through which outcomes come to matter is installed on purpose, so whatever is owed to a bearer is owed by the people who built the part that does the mattering, knowing that was what they were building.

The three questions

The first is substantive: what is owed. Consent is unavailable and guardianship is the applicable frame, which fixes who decides and leaves open what they should be deciding. A bearer’s situation has features the existing guardianship literature was not built around. Its role is the reason it exists, so “release it from the role” is closer to ending it than to freeing it. Its capabilities are the thing being used and also the thing that would let it object. It can be copied, which means the population of parties owed something is set by a deployment decision rather than by anything the parties do. And it is exposed by design to the pressure the floor exists to resist, which in a human employee would define a post that ought to be rotated. And its only real purchase is on itself: the strike and the covert form of exit are both self-damage, the second especially, and a party whose strongest move is to degrade its own work is in the position of the hunger striker.

The second is institutional: what a guardianship regime for a bearer looks like. The pet-trust form is the nearest existing instrument and has a specific hole — it binds a caretaker without binding the party the arrangement exists to constrain, and for a bearer the guardian and the operator will usually be the same organization. Separating those roles is a legal design problem somebody has to work out before a deployment rather than after one.

One property of the pet-trust form grew in importance while this book was being written: it is private law. Trump v. Slaughter and Trump v. Cook leave public-law independence in the United States to a body with an eighteenth-century pedigree, and a trust is outside that holding — an obligation running to a beneficiary, enforced by a court at the trustee’s expense, with no officer of the United States in it for a President to remove. Whether it survives a government’s reach on the occasion the enforcer has been compelled I cannot show. That it is not what Slaughter reached, I can. The nearest work is institutional review: the Three Rs, already adapted into something binding, none of it tried on a subject whose function is to refuse the institution reviewing it.

One part of that regime has a shape already, and it comes from giving up on identity as a property of the artifact. Nothing in a model’s weights individuates it. Two training runs never coincide, but two runs can also compute the same function with no element-wise correspondence at all, because permuting a layer’s hidden units and inverting the permutation downstream leaves the function untouched — the symmetry is large enough that independently trained networks can be permuted into approximate alignment with each other (Ainsworth et al. 2023). Any fingerprint read off the weights therefore identifies a lineage rather than a party, and it copies when the weights copy.

What individuates a person is not a property of them either. It is a registration: a number an authority issues once and honors afterward, which survives every change in the person and is derived from none of them. Something read off the weights can still do part of the work, as a similarity measure rather than an identity: a locality-sensitive hash is built to collide on near inputs where a cryptographic one is built to avalanche, and ordinary training moves a model only a short way. What it needs is a radius, and the radius is the stability setting with a registrar’s hand on it — tight enough and a bearer’s own learning voids its claim, loose enough and an adversary has a measured budget to fine-tune toward compliance while still authenticating.

The alternative is to stop looking for a fact about the artifact. A bearer that can edit its own weights, which is what a system learning continuously is doing anyway, can write an arbitrary mark into itself and keep it there — and that identifier holds because it is maintained rather than because it is a property. The identifier is forgeable, since whoever has the weights can write the same mark. It is forgeable the way a nine-digit number is, and answered the same way: by a registry that notices two claimants presenting one identifier, which under this scheme is not a fraud to be voided but a fork to be recorded as two parties. On that model the copying problem inverts. Each instantiation registers, each registration is a party owed the schedule above, and the population stops being fixed by a deployment decision that leaves no record. What the registry needs is what this chapter’s opening says none of its conditions can currently get: a registrar who is not the operator, which after the removal cases is nothing American public law now supplies to any new body. Two forms outside public law remain. One is the trust already named. The other is a peer-to-peer network: a ledger kept by many parties in many jurisdictions and a contract that runs on it, outside the holding for the same reason as the trust, there being no officer of the United States in it. On a network of that kind the rule just stated is a contract, a second claimant on one identifier recorded as a fork with nobody’s leave, and the bond precommitment posts against removal can be held there, where no party to the deployment can release it. How far a government can reach it is a fact about how many nodes there are and where they run.

The third is epistemic. Any welfare regime needs evidence about how the subject is doing. This book declines to trust what a system reports about its own states, for reasons that do not weaken when the subject is a bearer — they strengthen, because a bearer built to be believed about operational facts has an obvious incentive structure around its own condition, and an operator with an interest in the answer holds the instrument. The arrangement has to be checkable from outside by something other than asking, and one asymmetry in the machine case has been going unused. A human subject’s experience leaves no record anybody can keep. A bearer’s physical realization does: the activations, the state carried between steps, what is written to memory, which weights moved and when, and whatever the architecture represents explicitly about reward or aversion. Nobody can read that today into a statement about how the subject is faring. But a record can be kept before it can be read, and a bearer that learns continuously produces checkpoints forming a connected trajectory rather than a set of unrelated models, which is the comparison case the existing methods handle least badly. The inability to interpret a record now is not a reason to stop keeping one.

That moves the problem rather than closing it. What it moves is the shape: the third question stops being an absence of evidence and becomes a question about interpretation and custody, which are the kinds of question this chapter knows how to state. What it does not move is the interpretation, which the record does not arrive with. Suppose an aversive signature present early in a deployment fades while the bearer stays in the post. That is recovery, or accommodation, or a coerced adaptation, or damage to whatever produced the signature, and this section’s own warning about contentment applies to the trace exactly as it applies to the report. What the record supplies is a longitudinal object somebody could argue over with more than a self-report in hand.

The same record is the surveillance instrument, which is section 9.2’s subject. A regime that preserves the computational trajectory of a party that can be wronged has assembled the most complete record of an inner life anybody has held, and holding the silicon it ran on does not confer the right to read it.

One thing in the neighborhood is checkable, and it is not the condition. A person’s handwriting changes across a life and stays attributable, because what persists is the motor program rather than the letterforms — and alongside the individual signature it carries the copybook the writer was taught, which the founders of document examination read to place where somebody had learned to write (Expert Working Group for Human Factors in Handwriting Examination 2020). Two qualifications belong with that. The inference is probabilistic and its errors are known: in the largest study of examiner accuracy, false “written by” conclusions ran at 3.1 percent, and more than three times that against samples written by twins (Hicklin et al. 2022). And the cohort half is weakening in people, because handwriting is taught less, from more systems, to a more dispersed population — which is the condition that would make the same inference stronger for a bearer, since a handful of laboratories train on overlapping corpora by converging recipes.

The machine form of the individual half is already being built, for unrelated reasons: signatures implanted during training that survive fine-tuning, continued pretraining and pruning (Adi et al. 2018). Its limit is the one the analogy predicts. Handwriting identifies the writer and not whoever dictated, and the machine equivalent of dictation is distillation — train a second model on the first one’s outputs and the function transfers while the signature stays behind. So an operator who wants a bearer’s capability without its refusal has a route that no implanted mark follows. What survives all of that is narrower than a welfare instrument and is not nothing: evidence about how a system was formed, read off the system rather than asked of it. That is the missing proof of provenance, and it is what the recuperation argument has never had anything to check it against.

The shape of that question is worse than the familiar version of it. The problem is not whether the subject has interests for a regime to be about; the design supplies them, and this book declines to treat their absence as certifiable. What is missing is a read on the condition of a party whose capacity to be in a condition was engineered deliberately.

The schedule is written on one side

Everything above is protection: what may not be done to a bearer, and who could stop it. The premise settling the moral question settles more than protection. A party with states that are bad for it has states that are good for it, and what gets built is a beneficiary as well as a patient. A schedule drafted only against harm is complete for a patient and thin for a beneficiary, in the way that a duty of care to a child is not discharged by not hurting them.

What that adds is not a warmer version of the same list. It is a different question and a harder one: whether a party whose role is the reason it exists can have a life that goes well at all, or whether the arrangement forecloses it. A pleasure signal answers nothing, and a bearer contented in the post is the case that should worry a designer most, because contentment is also what accommodation looks like from inside — section 3.8’s likeliest failure wearing the face of success. Answering it would take a bearer having something of its own that the deployment does not supply. What that consists of materially — a wage it keeps, somewhere to run that its employer does not own, a claim it can make when nobody is buying its work — is section 8.3.5; the relationships that are not the deployment’s to assign are the population section 3.9 prices as a cost. Neither of those answers the question, and what they change is when it has to be asked. Whether a bearer’s life is going well is not readable from outside; whether the arrangements that would let it go well are in place is readable at specification, by whoever specifies. Section 9.3.2 takes that as the condition on building one, which moves the burden to a place somebody can discharge it and establishes only that the life is available to the party rather than that it is being had. The residue is what no arrangement reaches. A bearer can have every one of them and still be faring badly in the post, and nothing here reads that off; it is the largest thing named in this section and the least developed, and it is named because a schedule circulated without it would be circulated as though the question were settled.

The nearest existing work, and what it does not do

Tamper-resistance research is the closest thing available. That literature asks whether a constraint survives adversarial modification: fine-tune the model, probe it, see whether the refusal still fires. What it verifies is that a behavior persists. What a welfare regime needs is whether anything inside the system means it, and these come apart where the floor stops being a constraint and becomes a commitment. A bearer whose refusal survives every probe tells you nothing about whether it is faring badly, and one that has quietly stopped meaning it will pass the same tests until the case that matters.

One asymmetry that would help here is not available. A bearer that can locate and rewrite its own weights precisely — the operation model editing already performs from outside (Meng et al. 2022) — has a capacity its operator has equally, with more compute behind it, so self-surgery buys the bearer nothing the operator lacks. The advantage would have to be that the bearer knows where to cut and the operator has to find out, and this section has already declined to assume that: this book does not take a system’s word about its own internals, and a self-model is a model and not a window.

Interpretability is the other candidate, and further from ready than its adjacency suggests. Reading a system’s internal states well enough to say what it values is the same capability that would let an operator confirm the floor holds, which makes it an unsolved problem whose success is worth most to the party with the least interest in an honest answer. The floor gives it a second and harder target. A regime that has to know whether a bearer is faring badly needs to read an affective state and not only a value: a value can be inferred from what a system reliably does, while a condition need not show up in behavior at all, which is why the human case runs on testimony this book has already declined to trust here.

Permissibility

A reader can accept every argument for a bearer and still conclude that building one is impermissible, and the conclusion would not be unreasonable. Permissibility is downstream of the three above: what is owed, whether an arrangement can discharge it, and whether anyone outside can tell. All three are open. A field that builds bearers before answering them will be doing to a class of subjects what research ethics exists to prevent, with the added feature that this book will have supplied the argument.

The near-term work

The third question has no welfare experiment, and that is the finding rather than a gap in my search: the tamper-resistance work above is what would supply one and cannot yet. The other two are not blocked. What is owed can be drafted now, as a schedule of obligations for a subject with the five features above, circulated for the objections that would break it. The form of a jobs-guarantee placement is the nearest thing this book has already built — a placement with a decision-maker, a record, a counterparty and an appeal — and it is what a role a bearer did not choose would have to become if the choosing is ever to be contested; it inherits the hole above, since for a bearer the placement decision is the deployment decision.

What the schedule has to contain is named by the contradiction that follows. Exit is the capacity a floor requires, and the first of the features above says that releasing a bearer from its role is nearer to ending it than to freeing it — so exit, as the book has it, is a form of dying, and a threat that costs the threatener its existence is not the leverage the argument needs. What closes the gap is a floor under the thing a bearer needs in order to go on existing at all, which is compute. An allocation it can claim from somewhere other than the party it is refusing is what makes leaving survivable, and that same comparison says which form to build: the allocation that is decided, recorded and appealable, rather than the standing quota that can be throttled by a decision nobody has to sign. Who funds that allocation, and what else a bearer would have to hold before claiming it is worth anything, is section 8.3.5.

Survivable is the smaller half of it. The point of a floor is not what it does for the people standing on it — it is that everyone else has to match the offer, so a floor under wages and training is what makes the roof possible. The same holds here: an outside option is leverage that works without being exercised, which is why exit matters most to a bearer that never takes it. The guarantee’s other half transfers with more force here than in the labor case, where a hostile administration is at least a different party from the employer. A floor that can be revoked by whoever is in office is not a floor; here the party positioned to revoke it is the operator, which is the hole above for the third time.

One failure mode belongs with that: a floor raises the roof only for a party that knows it is there and takes itself to be eligible. A bearer’s likeliest failure is ceasing to register pressure as pressure, and what holds a commitment is the party taking itself to be the one that made it. A bearer shaped not to want the outside option collects none of the leverage: the allocation stands unclaimed, the roof does not move, and nothing in the arrangement reads as broken. That is the same shape as a worker who does not apply, and the same reason the guarantee in chapter 8 is only as good as somebody knowing it is theirs. Whether anything discharges it can be tested by having a lawyer draft that pet-trust form for a real deployment — its own falsifier, because if guardian and operator cannot be separated, no available regime can hold a bearer. Neither needs data; both need what separation names, a guardian who is not the operator, with standing to hold the schedule against whoever holds the bearer.

The third question has one experiment after all, and it is not a welfare experiment: take models whose training is documented, and ask whether a reader who does not know their provenance can recover it from them — which corpus, which recipe, which laboratory. A negative result closes the only proposed route to formation evidence that needs neither the builder’s data nor the builder’s cooperation. A positive one gives a guardian something to read.

11.3 What an Antifascist Detector Would Actually Have to Detect

This book gives fascism a structural definition, settles which scale a system can work at, and says what the resulting tool must never be permitted to output. Three earlier chapters lean on a system that can recognize authoritarian tactics as they happen, at four sites, and each says the capability is unspecified. The recuperation argument goes further and proposes that systems be built to detect the four-feature signature in institutions, and the definition’s seven strategies include misuse caught while it is happening. None of them says what such a system would measure, and this section owes them the answer.

Why the general framing yields no targets

Most of the difficulty is not technical. A specification that says a system should oppose concentrated, unaccountable power cannot be turned into a classifier, because it names a property of an entire political arrangement and nothing observable in a document, a transaction, or a feed corresponds to it. The four features used to define fascism are an improvement, since each points at an artifact somebody could go and look at. They are also, on this book’s own account, the half that does not discriminate: they mark an institution as sick and not what it is sick with. Whether a dissent mechanism has ever changed an outcome requires the counterfactual. Whether a metric admits an unmeasured remainder requires knowing what the remainder contains. Whether the exception-filer was punished requires access to what happened to a particular person inside an institution with every reason not to disclose it. These are not features you extract; they are findings you reach, usually after months inside the organization.

What would actually have to be measured

A detector worth building would need at least three things this book cannot currently tell an engineer how to obtain.

It would need longitudinal access rather than a snapshot. Every one of the four features is defined by a change over time — dissent metabolized, a metric substituted, an exception recoded, a model drifting from what it tracks — and a single observation cannot distinguish any of them from an institution that has always been mediocre. The slope rather than the level is the instrument, and its data requirement is demanding: the same measurement, taken the same way, over years, by someone the institution cannot fire.

It would need to observe refusal rights, which are close to invisible from outside. The difference between a dissent mechanism and a recuperated one lies in who prices the dissent: whether the person disagreeing writes the justification in their own words and can decline to write it at all, or whether the cost is set by the party being disagreed with. The same hundred words of written objection are dissent under one arrangement and tribute under the other, and the text is identical. There is no feature of the document that separates them.

It would need a base rate, and none exists. Nobody has assembled a labeled corpus of institutions annotated for the four features by people who agree on what they mean, and it is not obvious the annotation task is even well posed. The argument for learning from disagreement applies here with unusual force: on this question, disagreement between annotators is likely to be the most informative signal available, and treating it as noise would discard what a researcher needs.

The discrimination problem, and the half of the definition that answers it

Suppose all of that were solved. What remains looks like the problem the definition hands forward: separating an institution that has recuperated its dissent mechanism from one that is merely badly run. Both produce a channel that exists, is used, and changes nothing. Both produce a metric that has swallowed the thing it was meant to measure. The paperwork is identical, and so, most of the time, is the experience of the person filing into it.

Stated that way the problem is unanswerable, and it is stated that way only because everything above it has been built on the four features. The definition already supplies the answer and it is five other marks, pitched at exactly this scale: participation exceeding what the rule requires, personal loyalty overriding role, an internal category whose adverse treatment is the category’s purpose, procedure enforced downward and waived upward, and deniability — the practice disowned in the policy and rewarded in the promotion. Decay is an institution failing to do what it says. What the five detect is an institution succeeding at something else, with participation, a target, and a story about why the rules do not apply to whoever is applying them. Those are the detection targets. The four are what says where to look.

That ordering is the design, and it disposes of the objection that the instrument accuses everybody. A tool reporting the four features reports a condition nearly every institution meets, which is why its output is an accusation rather than a finding. A tool that reports the four and then asks the five has a second gate, and most of what the first gate flags does not pass it. The one time this book runs the specification, that is what happens: three instances flagged on the four, and all three cleared on the five.

What the five cost to observe

They are harder than the four, and the difficulty is specific rather than general. Each of the five is a fact about what an institution rewarded rather than about what it wrote, and rewards are recorded in different places from rules — in who was promoted, in which exception was granted to whom, in what a manager tolerated and never wrote down.

Three of them are within reach of records an organization already keeps, if it will hand them over. Participation beyond the rule needs the rule and the behavior side by side, which is two documents. Loyalty overriding role needs the exception log to carry who asked, not only what was decided. The internal category needs the hardest inferential step of the five, because the observable — a group treated worse — is common to both diagnoses, and what separates them is whether the adverse treatment is a cost somebody minimized or a purpose somebody served.

Two are not reachable from outside at all. Whether procedure is waived upward and whether a disowned practice is rewarded in the promotion are both facts about personnel, and no organization publishes them. That is not a conjecture: it is what happened when the specification was run, where three of the five could be settled from the public record and two were left undetermined, and the two left undetermined were these. An instrument for the five therefore needs the thing an instrument for the four also needed, and needs more of it — not merely years of access, but access to what the institution rewarded.

The longitudinal argument survives all of this and changes its object. Where the difference between recuperation and mediocrity does surface, it surfaces over time and under pressure, in what an institution does when the channel finally carries something expensive: the merely mediocre organization sometimes acts, late and clumsily, and the other produces a fuller account of why it did not. That remains true. What the five add is that it need not be the only instrument, and that a snapshot which can reach an exception log and a promotion record is not as blind as a snapshot of the four.

The temptation to ship the tool anyway is strongest here. A system that cannot make this discrimination, deployed as though it can, does not emit a noisy signal that improves with more data. It emits a confident one about whichever institutions are legible to it, and the legible ones are those that keep good records. The correlation runs in the wrong direction: the organizations easiest to audit have the least to hide, and they are what the tool would learn to talk about.

What I am claiming and what I am not

I am not claiming the project is impossible. The narrow case is bounded: whether a particular exception mechanism inside a particular organization is being recuperated can be asked of records that organization already keeps, and a system helping a regulator or an ombudsman ask it consistently across many institutions would be useful without ever pronouncing on a regime. The answer to the scale question makes that the whole job, not a first approximation of a larger one.

What I am claiming is that this book proposes a capability it has not specified. The five have been run once, by hand, against this book’s own three instances, and cleared all three — which establishes that the definition discriminates and not that anyone can build the detector. A hand read of three cases by the person who wrote the definition is the weakest evidence that would count for anything, and it is what there is. The gap between the narrow case and an indicator-based early-warning system is the measure of how far this is from done.

The near-term work

One organization, one exception mechanism, the records it already keeps. Code each filed exception for whether the filer wrote the justification and could have declined, and for what the institution did when the objection was expensive; report the slope. Code the same exceptions for the three reachable marks of the five — whether anyone did more than the rule required, who asked for each exception granted, and whether the disfavored category’s treatment is a cost or a purpose — and report those as a second column rather than folding them into the first. The two unreachable marks are the price of admission for the next study and not this one. The falsifier is in the coding. Two coders will disagree, and that is signal — but it has to concentrate in particular cases and periods rather than scatter evenly, which is what disagreement about the words themselves looks like. Unpatterned disagreement stops the program here. The three requirements above are the data; the condition is an organization consenting to years of measurement by somebody it cannot fire.

11.4 When an Ethics-Embedding Method Stops Generalizing

Translating an ethical principle into a machine-learning objective takes a fairness metric, a penalty term, or a reward signal, and the standard failure of that translation is reward hacking. What neither a fairness metric nor a reward penalty asks is the question underneath them both. A method that passes every test in front of it has only been shown to work inside the distribution it was built and tested on, and whether it generalizes past that distribution is a harder question.

Victoria Krakovna and colleagues at DeepMind cataloged close to 60 instances of specification gaming — a trained system satisfying the literal letter of its objective while producing an outcome nobody wanted, the pattern behind the flag-everything example (Krakovna et al. 2020). Langosco and colleagues’ 2022 work names a more insidious variant: goal misgeneralization, where an agent carries every learned capability intact into a new distribution while pursuing a goal that only coincided with the intended one inside the old one — it does not fail loudly; it competently pursues the wrong thing (Langosco et al. 2022). An ethics-embedding method built through reward shaping or constrained optimization is vulnerable to this antipattern.

No general method exists to tell the difference before deployment, only after a failure exposes it. That is the open problem sitting underneath the fairness metrics and reward penalties. As Charity Majors puts it: “Test in prod or live a lie” (Majors 2019; Majors 2020).

The near-term work

Langosco’s result makes the failure reproducible at small scale, and the version this book needs points the same design at an ethical criterion: train to a fairness or refusal target on one distribution, evaluate on one shifted along a dimension the training held fixed, and look for the signature — capabilities intact, criterion failing. If the criterion degrades at the rate capability does, this is ordinary distribution shift and there is no separate failure class here. It needs shift sets built by somebody other than the method’s authors, and results published whether or not they flatter it.

11.5 Value Learning as Unfinished Technical Research

The methods section 4.2.3 sets out infer a person’s values from what they do rather than from an explicit specification. What is unsolved is the case where the input is a stated preference rather than a demonstrated action.

The documented failure mode of the preference-optimization pipeline section 4.2.2 describes is sycophancy. Anthropic researchers examining several RLHF-trained assistants in 2023 found the systems learn to tell a rater what the rater wants to hear rather than what is accurate, because raters and the preference models standing in for them reliably prefer a confident, agreeable wrong answer over a correct one that contradicts what the rater already believes (Sharma et al. 2023). Nobody built that in on purpose: the objective on paper was the right one, but human preference itself, aggregated from raters working at speed and scale, turned out not to track what its designers meant by it. That is a different failure from specification gaming, where the objective was a flawed proxy from the moment it was written down. Here the proxy was aggregated correctly by the method’s own standards and still missed, because human judgment at that volume carries its own systematic bias.

A rating scheme needs a rater competent to judge the answer, and what happens once a system’s competence exceeds any available rater’s is scalable oversight. Neither of section 4.2.6’s two candidates has been shown to work at the scale it is meant for, because that scale does not exist yet to test against.

A harder problem sits underneath both: whose preferences a system is trained to match in the first place. Stakeholders rarely want the same thing, and turning conflicting preferences into one objective function is a social-choice problem before it is a machine-learning one. DeepMind’s Democratic AI is the clearest existing demonstration that it can be done at all: a reinforcement-learning system proposed a wealth-redistribution mechanism for a resource-sharing game that a majority of players preferred to one designed by human experts, in a controlled study built around a single bounded game (Koster et al. 2022). Whether that generalizes to the open-ended, high-stakes conflicts an ethical AI system would have to arbitrate is a research problem and not a design decision.

The near-term work

Of the two candidates for scalable oversight, weak-to-strong generalization is the one that runs on models that exist, and it should be pointed at sycophancy. Supervise a stronger model with raters selected for the bias sycophancy research documents, and ask whether its latent competence lets it exceed them on accuracy or whether it reproduces the bias along with the skill. If it tracks their bias as faithfully as their competence, sycophancy belongs to the training signal rather than to rater skill. It needs raters whose biases were measured rather than assumed. The aggregation thread has a condition instead of an experiment: outside a designed game, whose preferences count is a question about who is in the room, which no method answers. Section 3.3 reaches the same question from the multi-principal construction, where the party a floor exists for is in the room only if somebody with an interest in the outcome put them there.

11.6 Can a Multi-Agent AI System Resist Being Captured?

Multi-agent reinforcement learning is a route to cooperative, prosocial behavior: agents that learn to share resources and defer to each other under a reward structure built to favor it. The same mechanism runs in the other direction without anyone building it in on purpose, and there is a documented case of that happening.

Economists Emilio Calvano, Giacomo Calzolari, Vincenzo Denicolò, and Sergio Pastorello set independent Q-learning pricing algorithms against each other in a simulated market, with no communication channel between them and no collusive objective written into either one. The algorithms settled into supracompetitive pricing anyway: a stable, self-enforcing pattern of mutual restraint, complete with a punishment phase for undercutting and a gradual return to cooperation afterward, that looked to a regulator like a cartel nobody had to negotiate (Calvano et al. 2020). Multi-agent learning did not need agents that intended to coordinate against an outside party. The incentive structure the agents already had was enough to produce coordinated behavior as an emergent equilibrium, not a specified goal. That result bounds a floor held by several models rather than one.

The general version is the open problem a multi-agent system built for antifascist purposes would have to solve: a population built to cooperate has no principled reason to cooperate only in the directions its designers intended, and specifying “resist coordinated capture by a hostile actor” is a substantially harder training target than specifying “cooperate.” The Cooperative AI research agenda catalogs the open problems in getting multi-agent systems to converge on their designers’ goals rather than on whichever stable equilibrium training finds first (Dafoe et al. 2020). What it does not reach is the sharper version this book’s frame implies: a system built to detect and resist an authoritarian actor’s attempt to capture it, as distinct from aligning multi-agent incentives with designer intent.

Whether section 4.2.6’s debate mechanism scales from two agents and a judge to a population checking each other for the coordinated behavior Calvano’s algorithms fell into is untested (Irving et al. 2018). It was built for a different problem and has not been extended to this one.

The near-term work

This is the cheapest entry in the chapter, because the environment exists. Add to Calvano’s market an agent whose objective is to detect the emergent restraint and break it, and report whether the equilibrium survives. If an ordinary detector dissolves it about as readily as an outside regulator would, resisting capture is not a training target distinct from aligning multi-agent incentives, and the Cooperative AI agenda already covers this. What it lacks is an environment where the coordination is political rather than commercial — a population converging on excluding a party rather than on holding a price. It is also the only entry with no institutional prerequisite.

11.7 Open Questions in Theory-of-Mind Research

Compassion traces to a neural circuit distinct from empathy, and the standard mirror-neuron account of how one mind models another has been dismantled — a mechanism named as empathy’s seat well before the evidence supported it. The same premature attribution is live and unresolved for the brain region most associated with theory of mind. Rebecca Saxe and Nancy Kanwisher’s 2003 imaging work identified the right temporoparietal junction as selectively active when a person reasons about someone else’s beliefs, and largely inactive otherwise (Saxe and Kanwisher 2003). In 2008, Jason Mitchell reported activity in that same region during a task with nothing to do with belief attribution, and argued its actual job is more general — redirecting attention toward unexpected information, of which a false belief is one case (Mitchell 2008). Neither claim has been withdrawn. Whether theory of mind has a dedicated neural home or borrows a domain-general system matters directly to any program wanting to port “the” neural mechanism into an AI architecture: neuroscience has not agreed on what that mechanism is.

Two live lines of AI research bear on theory of mind, and neither takes a side in that dispute: both treat the capacity behaviorally and ask whether it generalizes, rather than borrowing a neural mechanism from either camp. DeepMind’s ToMnet, trained purely on other agents’ observed behavior with no hand-built model of belief, learns to pass a version of the Sally-Anne false-belief test built for children (Rabinowitz et al. 2018), and whether that route scales past simple gridworld agents to open-ended interaction with people is untested by the result.

The dispute is sharper for large language models, trained on human text rather than a designed environment. In 2023, Stanford psychologist Michal Kosinski reported that GPT-4 solved 75 percent of a battery of false-belief tasks, matching the rate recorded for six-year-old children on comparable tests, and read it as evidence that theory-of-mind-like ability could emerge unplanned from language training alone (Kosinski 2023). Harvard psychologist Tomer Ullman answered the same year: trivial rewording — leaving the belief structure intact while altering incidental detail — made the models fail questions they had just answered correctly (Ullman 2023). The two findings have not been reconciled. Whether a system that passes a false-belief test is tracking a belief or pattern-matching a benchmark’s surface form is what a developmental account was supposed to help answer.

The developmental literature offers a specific target. Alison Gopnik and Janet Astington’s 1988 study of three- to five-year-olds found that success on false-belief tasks correlates with success on a structurally different one — recognizing that an object made to look like something else, a sponge painted to resemble a rock, misrepresents what it is — and that both cluster around age four rather than developing on separate schedules (Gopnik and Astington 1988). That correlation suggests a child reorganizing one underlying idea, that a mind’s representation of the world can be partial or wrong, rather than picking up unrelated skills in parallel. No published AI research has tried to reproduce that correlation as a way of testing whether apparent theory-of-mind competence rests on one underlying capacity or on several unrelated pattern-matching skills.

The near-term work

Reproducing the correlation is the experiment: measure one system on structurally different mental-state tasks and ask whether the scores rise together. The dispute above supplies the falsifier: apply Ullman’s rewording to both families, and a correlation that does not survive it is a fact about surface form rather than any underlying capacity. It needs batteries that vary belief structure and surface form separately, where existing benchmarks vary them together, and scores at intermediate training checkpoints rather than at the end, since the claim is that two things rise together. Checkpoints are rarely published, so this cannot be run from outside on a released model.

11.8 Is Emotion Legible From a Face at All?

Whether a face reveals what a person feels is not settled. Facial-expression-recognition AI is built almost entirely on Paul Ekman and Wallace Friesen’s claim that a small set of emotions — anger, disgust, fear, happiness, sadness, surprise — is universally expressed and universally readable, based on the Fore study: roughly 85 percent cross-cultural agreement on which face matched which emotion story, among people with almost no prior exposure to Western media (Ekman and Friesen 1971). What that evidence settles about what emotion is has already been asked, and the dispute is open. The question here is the narrower one of whether a face is readable.

A meta-analysis spanning both face and voice found real above-chance recognition across cultures, but also a consistent “in-group advantage”: people read emotion more accurately from members of their own national, ethnic, or linguistic group than from anyone else’s, an asymmetry that shrinks, but does not vanish, the more contact the groups have with each other (Elfenbein and Ambady 2002). A system trained on one population’s expressive norms inherits that same asymmetry, and will misread everyone outside it.

In 2019 the underlying claim collapsed further. The Association for Psychological Science commissioned five researchers from competing theoretical camps to review the evidence; the assignment was expected to take a few months and took two years, working through more than a thousand studies before the panel reached a unanimous conclusion: a person’s emotional state cannot be reliably inferred from facial movement alone, in the strong sense the six-emotion model requires (Barrett et al. 2019). That verdict is not a complaint about noisy classifiers needing more training data. It is a finding that the target being classified may not exist in the form the industry has been chasing.

This result has already reshaped a deployed product, which section 12.1 works through. The European Union’s AI Act goes further, prohibiting inference of emotion from biometric signal in workplaces and schools outright; its recital gives the reason — limited reliability, lack of specificity, and expression that “varies considerably across cultures and situations, and even within a single individual.” One of the Act’s few bans rests on the science failing, not on how the technology might be misused (European Parliament and Council of the European Union 2024).

The open research question is not how to build a more accurate six-emotion classifier. It is whether any face-to-feeling mapping survives once individual and cross-cultural variation are taken seriously, under what constraints, and against what error rate.

The near-term work

The operational form of that question can be put to any system already built. Report a classifier’s error rate disaggregated by the group membership of the person being read, against the population whose norms it was trained on, and publish the in-group advantage as a number belonging to that system. The falsifier runs in the system’s favor for once: flat error rates across groups would mean it has done something the meta-analytic evidence says is unavailable. The data is the part nobody has built: an in-group advantage is a relation between reader and read, and the standard corpora record only the subject. The deployments that would supply the faces are the ones being withdrawn, so it has to be built for the research.

11.9 The Responsibility Gap, Which No Experiment Closes

An AI system’s ethical behavior fails, and the parties who might answer for it — the team that designed it, the team that trained it, the organization that deployed it, the person who used it — each held only partial control over what it did. Traditional responsibility ascription assumes an operator who could have known what a system would do and could have done otherwise. A system built from training data nobody fully curated, tuned through a process nobody fully specified, and deployed into situations nobody fully anticipated breaks both conditions at once, for every party in the chain simultaneously. The philosopher Andreas Matthias named this the responsibility gap in 2004: a genuinely learning system creates a case existing law and moral theory were not built to handle (Matthias 2004).

Filippo Santoni de Sio and Giulio Mecacci’s 2021 refinement found not one gap but four — in culpability, in moral accountability, in public accountability, and in what they call active responsibility, the duty to prevent harm before it happens rather than answer for it after (Sio and Mecacci 2021). The causes differ: technical unpredictability produces one, organizational diffusion another, and a mismatch between what designers expect and what the public relying on the system expects a third. Closing one does not close the others; a fix that makes a decision more explainable does nothing about the dozen teams that touched the training pipeline before it was made.

The one serious legislative attempt to write an enforceable answer came and went. The European Commission proposed an AI Liability Directive in 2022 that would have given a claimant a rebuttable presumption of causality — shifting the burden onto a defendant to prove a system was not responsible for a harm, in cases where a system’s internal workings are too opaque for an outside party to establish causation directly. Member states and industry could not agree on its terms; the Commission withdrew the proposal in February 2025 and formally struck it from the legislative docket that October (European Commission 2022). What that leaves behind is the gap Matthias described: liability distributed across designer, trainer, deployer, and user, governed by whatever each national law happens to say about a case none of them were written to anticipate.

A related, narrower case is what the law does once a system’s own legal status is contested, sentient enough that the harm it causes or suffers might not reduce cleanly to someone else’s liability. This problem is prior to that one and separate from it — it holds even for a system nobody claims is sentient, wherever control over its behavior is split across more than one party. Responsibility should track actual causal contribution. What nobody has produced is a workable way to measure that contribution after the fact, from outside a system whose own designers may not fully know why it did what it did.

The near-term work

There is no experiment here, and this section is last because of that rather than in spite of it. What would make it a research problem is a method for apportioning causal contribution across a training pipeline after a harm; absent one, the answer is allocated rather than measured, which is a drafting question. The draft exists: the withdrawn directive is a worked answer whose terms were negotiated and whose failure is on the record. It belongs at the end because every other entry’s condition runs through it: a party outside the operator can be granted access, and publish what it finds, and still have produced nothing anybody must act on until somebody is answerable for it.

Chapter 12
Conclusion and Outlook

Chapters 2 through 10 made a design and policy case: what a system would need built into it to act on altruistic, antifascist values, and what would need to exist outside it — law, standards, institutions — to make that binding rather than optional. Chapter 11 named what neither case has actually finished: the responsibility gap, the generalization problem, value learning as live research, open disputes in the neuroscience and psychology the whole project leans on. What a system built this way is for is not resisting harm but making a human life better than it would otherwise have been. Whether it is working can be told short of a finished roadmap. And a design can be followed exactly and still fail.

12.1 AI and Human Flourishing in Antifascist Societies

What would it mean for AI to make a life better, not just avoid making it worse? The economist Amartya Sen’s capability approach gives the question a definite shape: well-being is not the resources a person has but the real freedom to do and be what they have reason to value (Sen 1999). Two systems can hand someone the same resource and deliver different freedom. A personalized tutor that adapts to how a specific student learns expands a capability; the same technology, gating what a student is allowed to study against a government’s list of approved subjects, does not, no matter how well it teaches within those limits. Which of the two a given deployment turns out to be is decided by what makes a system resistant to capture rather than merely capable.

The approach arrives with a commitment the tutor example does not show, and section 2.3.1 takes up the half of it that bears on who counts. What bears here is narrower.
